Chapter 00

00Prologue: a polo field and a boycott

The short versionIn November 1993, Pearl Jam would not play Los Angeles on Ticketmaster's terms, so a small punk promoter called Goldenvoice put them on a polo field in Indio for about 25,000 people. The field belonged to a shopping-center heir who loved polo. The valley it sits in is named after a printer's typo. Six years later, the promoter came back with a festival.

The festival exists because a band would not pay Ticketmaster's service charges. On November 5, 1993, Pearl Jam played a polo field in Indio, two hours east of Los Angeles, to about 25,000 people.12 The promoter was Goldenvoice, a punk company from LA, and it was its first show on the grounds.3 Paul Tollett, one of the two men running it, has said that night showed him what the site could hold.4

He did not do anything about it for six years. But this is where the story starts: a grunge band in a fight, a promoter willing to drive, and a field that had never held a rock show.2

The fight

Pearl Jam wanted its tickets cheap, and the fight over that went fully public the following spring. In a memo to the Justice Department dated May 6, 1994, the band accused Ticketmaster of being a monopoly, and the department opened an investigation.5 On June 30, 1994, Stone Gossard and Jeff Ament testified to a House subcommittee about "the imposition of excessive service charges."6 They told Congress the band wanted tickets capped at $18 and service charges at 10%, printed separately from the price.6 Pearl Jam ended up cancelling its 1994 summer tour.5

The Indio show came seven months before the testimony, while the campaign was already under way.3 Ticketmaster's deals covered the big Southern California venues, so band management went looking for somewhere outside them. They picked a site Wikipedia's account of the tour describes as "untested and under-developed."2 NPR's version is simpler: Goldenvoice helped Pearl Jam find other places to play during the boycott, and one of them was the Empire Polo Club.4

The Ringer quotes Eddie Vedder on the move into the desert:

"You gotta run pretty far to get some space for yourself these days."3

The night itself

It was not a smooth gig. Five Horizons, the fan archive that logs every Pearl Jam show, notes cold weather and parking trouble, and a show that had first been planned for the LA area.1 The openers were Weapon of Choice, Eleven and American Music Club.1

The crowd threw shoes. They started during the openers. One hit Jack Irons' bass drum during Eleven's set, and he stopped the song.1 By the headline set the band was playing from behind its own amps to stay out of range.12

The lore · Shoe the shoeless

The story fans tell is that Vedder turned the barrage into a charity drive. The podcast Live on 4 Legs, which walks through Pearl Jam's shows one at a time, says he first threatened anyone who left barefoot, then told the crowd to keep throwing so the shoes could be given away - "shoe the shoeless."7 It's a fan-archive account, not a newspaper's. The shoes themselves are in every version: Five Horizons, Wikipedia and the podcast all have them.127

The man who founded Goldenvoice was not there. Gary Tovar had been arrested in March 1991 and was in prison by 1993, on marijuana charges.8 The company was being run by the two people he had handed it to: Paul Tollett and Rick Van Santen.3 Palm Springs Life credits the pair with making the deal for the show.9 The next chapter is about them.

The polo club

The Empire Polo Club was six years old in 1993. Alexander "Al" Haagen III founded it in 1987. He had taken up polo in 1983, and he was the patron, the player who owns and pays for a team, of the Empire team for 30 years.10 Under him the club hosted the first 40-goal tournament in the US, the top level of the sport, and the first US Women's Open.10

The money behind it was shopping centers. Haagen was vice chairman of Alexander Haagen Properties, the family's shopping-center developer, which built its first center in Rosemead in 1963.11 Palm Springs Life says the club was built as "a home for his favorite sport," with a dozen grass fields, two exercise tracks and stables.9 The Museum of Polo counts 11 fields.10 Polo people count fields differently depending on who is asking. Chapter 3 has the full tally.

Two details from the club's old life are still there every April. One is the rose garden along the south side of Field 3, the main Sunday polo field. The club's own site put it at about 8,000 bushes in 107 varieties, with a life-size bronze called She Gave Her Best by Rich Roenisch.12 The other is the grass. Polo needs it, and the club watered the fields 11 months a year. Goldenvoice took over the watering in April.13 Haagen explained the priority in 2015:

"Polo ponies run on grass. They don't run on dirt."13

That is the deal in one line. The festival happens on grass because horses need grass. Rock shows on dirt lots are a different, dustier business.

Tollett has never hidden how much the arrangement mattered. In 2021, when Goldenvoice took over the club year-round, he said "Goldenvoice has had a unique relationship with Empire Polo Club for over twenty-five years," and "It's immeasurable how much we've learned from Al Haagen."14 Twenty-five years back from 2021 is roughly the Pearl Jam show.

The name is a typo

The festival is called Coachella. It is not in Coachella.

The name started as a mistake. In 1901, a railroad worker named Jason Rector, who had cleared the trees at a spot known as Woodspur, proposed calling the new townsite "Conchilla," Spanish for "little shell," after the fossil shells in the ground.4 The developers sent the name off to be printed in their prospectus. It came back as "Coachella." Rather than hold up the announcement, they kept it.4 The town did not become a city until 1946.4

The historian Jeff Crider puts it plainly to NPR:

"'Coachella' was a mistake."4

The valley took the name, and the festival took it from the valley. Its full name is the Coachella Valley Music and Arts Festival, and it has been held in Indio, the next city over, since 1999.4 The city of Coachella is next door. Chapter 14 goes there.

Why Indio

Put the pieces together and the choice makes sense, even if nobody planned it.

  • It was outside the system. Pearl Jam needed a site that was not a Ticketmaster venue, and a private polo club was not one.24
  • It was already a big lawn. A dozen irrigated grass fields is a lot of room, and the grass gets watered whether there is a concert or not.913
  • It had an owner who said yes. The festival has been on Haagen's land since its first year, and Stagecoach since 2007.14
  • It had proof. About 25,000 people drove into the desert in November for one band.1 That was the number Tollett carried around for six years.

He was not counting on the heat, or on how few of them would come back the first time. That is the next chapter.

The lore · "The night that started Coachella"

This is the founding story Goldenvoice tells, and it's mostly true. Tollett has said the Pearl Jam show showed him the site could work for a festival.4 Wikipedia goes further and quotes Billboard, 2012, saying the concert "sowed the seeds" of Coachella.2 The documented version is a little slower. The first festival was in October 1999, six years later, and it nearly sank the company.3 The seed was real. It took a while to come up.

Still open

  • The original 1993 LA Times review (Steve Hochman, "Pearl Jam Blossoms in Desert," Nov 8, 1993) is behind a wall this project couldn't get past. If you have a copy, the attendance figure and any word on ticket prices would help.
  • Who first called Goldenvoice about the polo field, the band's management or Tollett? The sources credit both.

Chapter 01

01The beginning

The short versionGoldenvoice was a punk promoter paid for by its founder's marijuana smuggling, handed to two young employees when he went to prison. Their first festival, in October 1999, drew about half the crowd they needed and lost $850K to $1M. Paul Tollett sold his house. Then they skipped a year, came back for one day, sold the company to AEG, and kept adding days until two identical weekends grossed $114.6M in 2017.

The first Coachella was so hot that a DJ's records melted. Kid Koala, one of nine acts from the label Ninja Tune, remembered "the vicious 100-plus-degree heat" and said "my vinyl started melting at one point."3 That was October 1999, in Indio, at $50 a day.3 It lost about a million dollars.3 The company that put it on had started 18 years earlier, as a way to spend smuggling money on punk shows.15

Punk money

Gary Tovar's first show was on December 4, 1981, at La Casa de la Raza in Santa Barbara: T.S.O.L., Shattered Faith and Rhino 39.15 He thought of Santa Barbara as the minor leagues, a place to practice "before I came into the majors."15 The company's name came from a favorite strain of Thai marijuana.8

That was not a joke. Tovar had been trafficking marijuana for about a decade before Goldenvoice existed, and OC Weekly says the pot money, "reaching into the double-digit millions," is what kept the shows running.15 They needed it. Tovar says he lost "$3 million to $4 million over 11 years," in 1980s dollars, roughly $300K to $400K a year.15 He told the Los Angeles Times how the two halves of his life fit:

"One of my hands was in punk rock... And my other hand was smuggling quality marijuana."8

Federal agents arrested him at his home on March 8, 1991.8 He pleaded guilty to four charges and served seven years in prisons in Arizona and Nevada.15 Before he went in, he signed Goldenvoice over to two of his employees, Paul Tollett and Rick Van Santen.15 (The Times says the handover happened while he was inside, and The Ringer says shortly before. The result is the same.)83

So the two men who booked Pearl Jam onto a polo field in 1993 were running a company they had been given, while its founder served his sentence.9 Tovar never stopped turning up. He is a consultant to Goldenvoice now, and he has not missed a Coachella since he got out.8

The pitch: "dance music is coming"

The Ringer has the best scene from the planning. Perry Farrell says Van Santen asked him in a Santa Monica hotel hot tub what he would do with a festival. His answer: "I was telling him dance music is coming."3 Farrell knew the risk. Of his own earlier Enit Festival, he said: "I did Enit, I lost a million dollars. And that was my own money."3

The bill Goldenvoice built for 1999 took the advice. Beck, Tool and Rage Against the Machine headlined, with the Chemical Brothers, Morrissey, A Perfect Circle, Jurassic 5 and Underworld underneath.16 Ninja Tune alone sent nine acts.3 The DJs got a tent of their own, and by 2002 the Sahara tent already had a laser show projected onto circular screens hung from its ceiling.17

October 1999

The first Coachella Valley Music and Arts Festival ran Saturday and Sunday, October 9-10, 1999, at the Empire Polo Club.3 Tickets were "a then-daunting $50 per day, plus fees."3

The timing was bad. The infamous Woodstock '99 had happened just two months earlier, and GRAMMY.com notes how much skepticism that left behind.1616 The weather was worse. October in Indio was still over 100 degrees.3

The crowd was about 17,000 to 20,000 a day, which The Ringer calls "barely more than half" of what Goldenvoice had hoped for.3 Wikipedia's figures make it about 37,000 tickets across both days, against a goal of 70,000.18 Far Out uses the same 37,000.19

By the numbers · October 1999

  • Dates: Oct 9-10, two days3
  • Price: $50 a day, plus fees3
  • Heat: over 100 degrees3
  • Crowd: about 17,000-20,000 a day3
  • Goal: about 70,000 total, per Wikipedia18
  • Loss: $850K (GRAMMY.com) to "around a million dollars" (Tollett, via The Ringer)163

"We knew we were dust"

The loss depends on who is counting. GRAMMY.com puts it at $850,000.16 Tollett, in a 2017 New Yorker profile quoted by The Ringer, put it at "around a million dollars."3 mxdwn's summary of the same profile gives the range, $850K to $1M, and says it nearly bankrupted Goldenvoice.20 Tollett's own summary is three words long:

"We knew we were dust."3

The bands helped. "A couple bands let us slide," Tollett said.3 The best-known of them is Rage Against the Machine. Tom Morello told al.com that "the festival lost so much money they asked us for our fee back, which we gave them because they were friends."16 Far Out reports it was half the fee, not all of it.19 Morello's own wording doesn't say which.

The lore · The house and the car

The founding legend of Coachella is that Paul Tollett sold his house to pay for the first one. This one holds up. Tollett told The New Yorker he was forced to sell his house and his car, as The Ringer and mxdwn both quote it.320 mxdwn's version is "his house and eventually his car," which makes it sound like the car went second.20 Palm Springs Life has the version he tells himself: "Tollett often says he lost his house, his car, and his wife."21 The third item is his joke to tell. The first two are on the record.

According to the same profile, it took the festival four years to make money.20

2000, the year off

There was no Coachella in 2000.16 The Ringer says only that the loss meant it "did not return in 2000 at all."3 Wikipedia adds that Goldenvoice had held October dates that year and cancelled them, with Tollett blaming too many festivals in Southern California.18 Nobody this project has read puts it more strongly than that.

2001: one day, in April, and a sale

When it came back, it was smaller and earlier. The 2001 festival was one day, Saturday April 28, and it moved to spring, when the desert is milder.224 The Ringer calls it a "pared-down one-day lineup."3 Jane's Addiction headlined, with Perry Farrell, who would play the first 12 Coachellas in one band or another.3

The rest of the bill came from both sides of the 1999 advice. Weezer, the Roots, Mos Def and Gang Starr were on it, and so were Fatboy Slim, Paul Oakenfold and the Chemical Brothers, who worked Black Sabbath's "Iron Man" into their set.23 Sigur Rós played their first Southern California show.23 The Daily Bruin counted 35,000 people; Wikipedia gives 32,000, at $65 a ticket.2318

Whether 2001 made money is a real disagreement. Wikipedia quotes Tollett estimating a loss of a "low, low six-figure sum."18 Far Out says it was the year the promoters could finally "make a profit."19 Tollett's line about four years to turn a profit points to the first.20

The bigger news that year was off the field. In 2001, Goldenvoice was sold to the live-music company AEG, Anschutz Entertainment Group.243 The price usually quoted is $7 million, for a sale agreed in March 2001. That figure comes from Wikipedia, and this project has not found the original report behind it.18 Tollett stayed in charge. In 2019 he described himself to the Los Angeles Times as "owner-operator of the show," and said: "I'm partners with AEG."24

Building it back up

The festival grew a piece at a time.

  • 2002: back to two days, with Björk second-to-last on the Saturday main stage, the Chemical Brothers closing it, and Oasis on Sunday.17
  • 2003: camping arrived. OC Weekly's history of the campgrounds calls 2004 "the second year camping was permitted."25 That December, Rick Van Santen died at home in Ventura County, aged 41, of flu-related complications.26 He was co-president of Goldenvoice.26
  • 2004: the first sellout.4 NPR puts the crowd at 120,000; the Las Vegas Sun reported about 100,000 over two days, about 50,000 a day.427 A two-day pass was $142.27 It was hot again: Saturday afternoon went past 100 degrees.27 That same year AEG bought half of Coachella, and Tollett kept the other half.3 Palm Springs Life credits the purchase to AEG's then-CEO Tim Leiweke.21 Wikipedia, citing the New Yorker profile, says the deal also gave AEG the controlling interest.18
  • 2007: three days for the first time, Friday April 27 to Sunday April 29, with Björk, Red Hot Chili Peppers and Rage Against the Machine on top.28 A three-day pass was $249, and single days, at $85, were "very limited."28 The first two days stayed on sale until the gates opened; the third, with Rage's reunion, "had sold out instantly."29 Stagecoach, Goldenvoice's country festival, opened on the same grounds that spring.14
  • 2008: the year Tollett says he lost the most money, with Prince headlining and Goldenvoice spending on Stagecoach at the same time.3 "I lost a lot of money in 2008," he told the Times in 2019.24

Too many people

By 2010 the problem had flipped. For the first time the festival sold only three-day passes, at $269, and all 75,000 sold out in advance.30 It still drew what the Las Vegas Sun later called "colossal crowds," with long entry lines and bad traffic.31 Tollett did not dodge it:

"The mistakes were in the planning."31

For 2011 Goldenvoice put a wrought-iron fence around the grounds to stop gate-crashers, paid toward an extra road lane in and out, and dropped the slow ticket-for-wristband swap.31 Tollett's summary of the fixes: "Fewer bodies. More land. Better traffic capabilities."31

Then, on May 31, 2011, he announced the move that defines the modern festival: in 2012 Coachella would run twice, on two weekends, with the same lineup.32 His reasons were demand and resale. "We had too many people who wanted to go," he told The Boombox, and he hated watching fans pay "$500-$600" on the secondary market.32 The obvious alternative was a bigger single weekend, and he turned it down:

"I didn't want to ruin the show by putting 40,000 more people in per day."32

It worked at once. When 2012 passes went on sale, weekend one sold out in about 40 minutes and weekend two "within another two hours."33 The two weekends earned $47.3M.34

The curve

From there the numbers come from Pollstar and Billboard, and they only go one way.

By the numbers · The gross, two-weekend era

  • 2012: $47.3M34
  • 2013: $67.2M35
  • 2014: $78.3M35
  • 2015: $84.3M, on 198,000 tickets36
  • 2016: $94.2M, on 198,000 tickets37
  • 2017: $114.6M, on 250,000 tickets38

The 2017 figure, $114,593,000 in Pollstar's year-end chart, made Coachella what GRAMMY.com, citing Billboard, called "the first reoccurring festival to break the $100 million mark in a single year."3834 (GRAMMY.com's headline calls it profit. It's a gross: ticket money in, before anyone gets paid.)34 Dancing Astronaut, also crediting Billboard, put it as "the only reoccurring festival to gross over $100 million."39 Billboard's own Power 100 the next year said Coachella "earned over $114.6 million in 2017."40

A two-day pass in 2004 cost $142.27 A pass in 2017 started at $399, according to Pollstar's range.38 The festival that could not find 70,000 people in 1999 sold 250,000 tickets in 2017.1838

Where the money goes is mostly the bill. Tollett told The New Yorker that each of the 2017 headliners, Radiohead, Lady Gaga and Kendrick Lamar, was paid $3-4M, and that the acts at the bottom of the poster got a little under $10,000.41 The 1999 headliners had let him slide on their fees. Eighteen years later, one headliner cost several times what the whole first festival lost.341

The partnership with AEG has not always been comfortable. When the Anschutz Foundation's donations to anti-LGBTQ groups became news in January 2017, Tollett told The New Yorker, as mxdwn reported: "I was offended. I run the festival, but it's rude to say that when you're a partner with someone."20 The money chapter picks that up.

No gross has been published since 2017. The Ringer, in 2019, could only say "well over $100 million" a year, at about 125,000 people a day.3 Chapter 2 has every year, including the ones with blank cells.

What it took

Read in order, the beginning is a run of near-misses. A company paid for by smuggling money. A founder in prison. Two employees given the keys. A first festival in October heat, two months after Woodstock '99, at a loss that cost one of them his house.15163 A year off. A one-day comeback. A sale to AEG.24

Van Santen did not see the sellout.264 Tovar did, and he is still there every April.8

Still open

  • The original source for the $7M AEG price. Contemporary reporting from March 2001 (LA Times, Billboard, Pollstar) would settle it.
  • Did 2001 lose money or make it? Tollett's figure and Far Out's disagree, and neither cites a document.
  • The 1999 attendance goal of 70,000 comes through Wikipedia. A 1999 Desert Sun or LA Times preview would confirm it.

Chapter 02

02Every year

The short versionEvery edition on one page, from Pearl Jam's polo-field show in 1993 to the 2026 sellout. A $50 day ticket became a $649 weekend, two days became six, and the gross climbed to $114.6M in 2017 before the published numbers stopped. Three years are missing entirely, and most recent years have no official attendance at all.

The first Coachella charged $50 a day and lost somewhere between $850,000 and a million dollars.316 Eighteen years later the same polo field grossed $114.6M, the first recurring festival to clear $100M.3839 The table below is everything in between, one row per year.

The shape of the curve

It did not go up in a straight line. It went up in steps, and each step was a format change.

Days. Two days in 1999. Nothing in 2000. One day in April 2001, headlined by Jane's Addiction.323 Two days again until 2007, when Coachella went to three days for the first time.28 Then, from 2012, the same three days twice: two weekends, identical lineups, sold separately.32

Prices. A two-day pass cost $142 in 2004.27 The first three-day pass, in 2007, was $249.28 It sat at $269 through the end of the one-weekend era.31 The two-weekend years start at $285 plus fees in 2012, then $349 in 2013, $429 in 2018 and $499 in 2024.42434445 For 2026, general admission started at $649 for the first weekend.46

Grosses. The published numbers run $67.2M in 2013, $78.3M in 2014, $84.3M in 2015, $94.2M in 2016 and $114.6M in 2017.35363738 That is the whole run of figures this project could tie to Billboard or Pollstar. Earlier years in the table come from Wikipedia's summary of Billboard Boxscore and are labeled that way. After 2017, nothing. The money chapter covers why that matters.

The bad years. 2000 was skipped after the first one lost money.3 2010 sold three-day passes only30 and still got overrun at the gates; LA Weekly reported gate-crashing, counterfeit tickets and three-hour entry lines.47 An Icelandic volcano's ash cloud stranded seven acts in England the same weekend.48 The fixes for 2011 were a wrought-iron fence, an extra road lane and the end of the ticket-for-wristband swap.31 Weeks after that festival, on May 31, 2011, Goldenvoice announced the second weekend.32

The gap. Coachella 2020 was moved to October, then cancelled by Riverside County's public health officer in June.4950 The same officer cancelled the April 2021 dates in January 2021.51 The festival came back in April 2022, so the table has two empty rows in a row for the first time.

The slump and the snap-back. In 2024, a first weekend that used to sell out in hours took nearly a month.45 For 2025, second-weekend passes were resold below face value.52 Then 2026 sold out both weekends three days after passes went on sale.53

How to read the table

  • Attendance and gross are as reported. Each cell says who reported it. Where two sources disagree, both are in the row.
  • Some years have no number. No attendance figure was found for 2018 through 2023, and no gross for 2018 onward. An empty cell means nobody published one, not that it was zero.
  • 125,000 is a ceiling, not a count. That is the daily cap Indio has allowed since a 2016 vote.54 It is not how many people came.
  • City counts are their own thing. For 2024, Indio counted 81,690 tickets for the first weekend and 80,148 for the second.55 Those are far below the cap, and may cover only the tickets the city collects a fee on.
  • Admissions are not people. 2014's 579,000 is not a typo. It is 96,500 a day across six days, the way Billboard counted it.35 The number of different people who came was closer to a third of that.
  • Prices are the cheapest general-admission pass at the time it went on sale, before fees. Prices were tiered from about 2022, so the same weekend often sold at more than one price.
  • 1993 is not a Coachella. It is the Pearl Jam show that put Goldenvoice on the polo field. It is in the table because the festival starts there.3

Every row links its sources. If you have a better one, especially a Boxscore printout or a city report, the corrections address is at the bottom of every page.

  • Iggy Pop reunited with Ron and Scott Asheton as the Stooges, their first show together in about 30 years. The Las Vegas Sun ranked it second-best of the weekend.
  • MCA gave an anti-war speech during the Beastie Boys' set, weeks after the invasion of Iraq, and the crowd reaction was mixed.
  • The White Stripes and Blur also played the main stage. Goldenvoice co-president Rick Van Santen died that December, aged 41, of flu-related complications.

2005

Dates
Apr 30-May 1, 2005
Headliners
Coldplay, Nine Inch Nails, New Order (Sunday closer after NIN, per Rolling Stone)
Attendance
about 50,000 per day expected (Las Vegas Sun); Rolling Stone estimated 50,000

  • Arcade Fire played Sunday on the Outdoor Stage. Rolling Stone called the set 'galvanizing' and said it drew a massive crowd.
  • Bauhaus played together for the first time since 1998, with Peter Murphy hanging upside down during 'Bela Lugosi's Dead'. Gang of Four's original lineup played its first North American show since 1981.
  • Coldplay's 'Yellow' turned into a crowd singalong.

Chapter 03

03The grounds

The short versionFor 22 years Coachella was a tenant, renting a polo club in Indio for a few weeks each spring and handing it back. Goldenvoice bought the land next door in 2012, and in 2021 took over the whole club year-round on a lease that runs to 2050. Somewhere between 78 and 1,000 acres, depending on who is counting, now flips from Coachella to Stagecoach in under a week.

For most of its life, Coachella was a tenant. Every spring Goldenvoice rented a private polo club in Indio, built a festival on the grass, took it down, and gave the grass back to the horses. Before October 2021, its arrangement with Empire Polo Club covered only the festival calendar.14 Then the tenant took the keys. Goldenvoice now runs the place all year, on a lease that runs through 2050.56

This chapter is about the land: who owns it, how much of it there is, what the city of Indio allows on it, and how one patch of desert turns into a country festival in a single week.

Whose polo club

Empire Polo Club was founded in 1987 by Alexander "Al" Haagen III, a player who had taken up the sport four years earlier and who would be patron of the Empire team for 30 years.10 The money behind it came from shopping centers: Haagen was vice chairman of his family's developer, Alexander Haagen Properties, and as a college-age construction supervisor had helped build its first center, in Rosemead, in 1963.11 One source credits plain "Alexander Haagen" with the founding, which could mean the father.9 The Polo Museum names the son, and so does Paul Tollett, who calls him "Al Haagen."14

Goldenvoice first came to the club in 1993, when Tollett and Rick Van Santen staged a Pearl Jam show on the polo grounds.9 It was Goldenvoice's first show at Empire, booked while the band was at the height of its fight with Ticketmaster.3 Coachella followed in 1999, and Stagecoach in 2007.14 By 2021, Tollett could say Goldenvoice had "a unique relationship with Empire Polo Club for over twenty-five years."14

The club's own address is 81-800 Avenue 51, Indio.57 Everything else in this chapter happens around it.

Buying the neighbors

In March 2012, Goldenvoice announced it had bought about 280 acres around the festival site.58 The package included Eldorado Polo Club, Triangle Bar Farms, Fish Creek and the property of Ambassador Glen Holden.59 The price was not disclosed.58

Tollett's reason was practical. The land would "help us put in some infrastructure so we don't have to keep coming back and doing the same things each year."58

The biggest piece, the 200-acre Eldorado Polo Club, belonged to Eldorado Lake Farms, a group of 19 player-owners.60 The festival had already been using it for parking and camping.5960 The deal came with a leaseback: polo would keep the fields each winter, roughly November 1 to early April.60 That arrangement is the hinge of the next chapter.

When did the money actually change hands? The 2012 reports said escrow would close in about 60 days.60 Wikipedia dates the purchase to 2013 and puts it at $30 million.18 A local blog post from July 2013 is headlined as Goldenvoice closing escrow on 200 acres for "$30+ million," but it could not be opened for this piece. The likeliest story is an announcement in 2012 and a close in 2013. Riverside County's deed records would settle it.

How big is it, exactly

Nobody agrees, and the disagreement is mostly about what counts.

By the numbers · The acreage, by source

  • 78 acres: the polo club proper, per Wikipedia18
  • About 642 acres: the event footprint with parking and camping, per Wikipedia18
  • "642-plus acre desert oasis": IQ Magazine, at the 2021 deal61
  • 1,000 acres: Wikipedia's page on the club itself62
  • "250 acres of usable event space": AEG's own sales page63
  • About 50 acres added in 2016, along Monroe Street, Avenue 50, Avenue 52 and Polo Road, per Wikipedia18

The field counts disagree too. The Polo Museum calls Empire an "11-field facility."10 Palm Springs Life says "a dozen grass polo fields."9 The US Polo Association (USPA) said 14 grass fields and two indoor arenas in 2016, a count that may include Empire's separate Calhoun Ranch fields.64

The honest summary: the polo club is the middle, the festival spills well past it, and the total depends on whether you count the parking lots. Nobody has published a map that matches stages to numbered polo fields.

The city's terms

Indio does not own the land, but it decides what happens on it, and most of the site's shape comes from a handful of votes.

The tax standoff. In July 2012 the city considered an admissions tax of 5-10% on events over 2,500 people. That would have added about $36 a ticket. Tollett said 2013 could be the last year in Indio, and that the festivals would skip 2014 and find a new home for 2015.65

The 2013 deal. A year later the council voted 4-0 to keep the festivals in Indio through at least 2030. Goldenvoice's per-ticket payment to the city rose from $2.33 to $5.01, starting in 2014. The deal allowed up to five festivals a year on the site.66 That clause is why Desert Trip and Power Trip could happen in October (see the next chapter).

The 2016 cap. Goldenvoice asked to grow. The council approved Coachella going from 99,000 to 125,000 people a day and Stagecoach from 75,000 to 85,000, phased in.5467 The mayor promised the increase would be "gradual over time."67 The cap is still 125,000 a day.68

Who actually shows up. The state's alcohol regulator, which sends 30-35 agents to each weekend, counted daily peaks of nearly 110,000 at Coachella and about 82,000 at Stagecoach in 2025.69 So the site runs below its legal ceiling, at least by that count.

The 2050 extension. In August 2021 the council voted 3-1 to extend the development agreement from 2030 to 2050. Councilmember Oscar Ortiz voted no, citing the length and the lack of community outreach. The attendance cap and the number of festivals did not change.70

What the city gets now. In 2024 Indio collected $2.658 "per ticket/per day," just over $2 million in total.55 How that squares with the $5.01 figure from 2013 is not obvious from the public record. The money chapter digs into it.

The curfew. The curfew is part of the contract with Indio, reported as 1 a.m. on Friday and Saturday and midnight on Sunday.71 Go five minutes over and the festival owes $20,000, then $1,000 for every minute after that, paid into the city's general fund.72

The year-round lease

On October 27, 2021, Goldenvoice announced it would take "year-round operational control" of Empire Polo Club under a long-term lease with the Haagen family. The deal also put Goldenvoice in charge of the Empire Grand Oasis in Thermal, and opened both properties to "special events, sporting events, and private functions."14 Press reports put the lease at through 2050, matching the city deal.56 Wikipedia calls it a 28-year lease, which lands in the same place.62

The agreement also allowed two additional large-scale festivals on the site every year.61

What it ended was polo. Some of the club's staff were laid off on three days' notice, and polo stopped being played at Empire.73 That story, and where the horses went, is the next chapter.

The beer gardens, and the end of them

For its first 23 years, Coachella kept its drinkers fenced in. You bought a beer inside a beer garden or the craft beer area and stayed there until you finished it. Guards at the exits made sure.74

That changed quietly. The Desert Sun dates a loose, unofficial start to 2023 and the first clear year of walk-around drinking to 2024. Staff check IDs and give anyone 21 or older a wristband, and bars are scattered across the grounds. The beer gardens are still there, but nobody stops you at the gate any more. A 16-ounce Heineken was $15.74 The Independent reported that the change followed complaints from 21-plus attendees.75

Stagecoach, on the same grass, has always let people walk with a drink.74

Why Coachella fenced alcohol in for so long is not on the record. It could have been a condition of the state liquor license, a term of Indio's permit, or Goldenvoice's own policy for an all-ages crowd. No source found for this piece says which.

The drinking spaces have their own history. Heineken has been the official beer since 2002; by 2014 it was in its 13th year.76 Heineken House replaced the sponsor's domes that year, with sets by Skrillex, Outkast and Lorde.76 In 2019 it was rebuilt with what its sponsor called a first-ever 21-plus beer garden, and De La Soul and The Roots played it.77 The Craft Beer Barn arrived in 2014, built by Tony Yanow of Tony's Darts Away and Mohawk Bend at the invitation of Nic Adler. It poured 120-150 beers from no more than 40 taps, and it was cash only.78

The rose garden

The oldest decoration on the grounds predates the festival. The Rose Garden Field, Field No. 3, was Empire's main Sunday polo field. Along its south side runs a garden of about 8,000 rose bushes in 107 varieties, with a life-size bronze called "She Gave Her Best" by Rich Roenisch.12 Today it is marketed as a three-acre venue with statues, fountains, three Victorian gazebos and a 5,000-square-foot indoor pavilion.79 Who planted it, and when, is not on the record.

At Coachella it is the VIP area: a walled-off garden of flowers, sculptures and palms, with air-conditioned restrooms and bars.80 Every night of both weekends, the traveling dinner series Outstanding in the Field sets a long table there for about 200 guests.81 In 2025 a seat cost $350, on top of the VIP pass.80

The garden has also been a neighbor to the noise. For years the Sahara, the dance tent, sat at the far edge of the grounds next to what became the Rose Garden VIP area.82 It stayed there until 2018.83

The Rose Garden is not part of Stagecoach.84

The landmarks

On a site this big, people navigate by the things that stick up.

The Ferris wheel. It belongs to Ray Cammack Shows, a Phoenix-area carnival family in business since 1963.85 It was in place by 2010; nobody has pinned down its first year.30 The current wheel, La Grande XL, replaced the original La Grande Wheel in 2017. It arrives on 20 semis and has 36 air-conditioned gondolas and more than 500,000 LEDs.86 The build depends on who you ask: a 70-ton crane, 10 crew and five days, says the company's vice president in a 2026 report;86 a 60-ton crane and about 10 days, says an undated Phoenix New Times piece, which also gives the wheel's height as 150 feet.85 The two may describe different wheels. The rest of the year it tours fairs and rodeos.86

Spectra. The seven-story spiral tower with an observation deck was commissioned in 2018 from Patrick O'Mahony's UK studio, Newsubstance. It was supposed to come down after 2020. Covid delays and fan affection kept it up, and it is now treated as a permanent feature of the festival.87 "I don't think there's any other festival in the world that would commission something like this," O'Mahony said.87 Whether it stands all year, through the summers and the soccer tournament, no source says outright.

The Sahara. Its concrete footing sits about two feet below the grass, permanent, while the tent above it has been put up and taken down each spring.82 In 2024 it moved for the second time, into a new steel structure between Avenue 51 and Polo Road, on about 1,500 tons of concrete poured overnight starting in December. Goldenvoice's Jason Brown called it a "whole new area of the venue that we've never been into before."83

Even the water features work. Medjhool Lake, on the property, also supplies the trucks that spray down the dust during the festival.13

The flip to Stagecoach

Coachella's Sunday crowd leaves, and the changeover starts behind them. It runs through Monday and the rest of the week, until the grounds are a country-western venue.88

Some things don't move. The Ferris wheel stays in the same spot for both festivals, and Spectra stays as a permanent installation.84 Everything else gets a costume change:

  • The Mojave tent becomes Palomino.
  • Yuma becomes Diplo's Honkytonk.
  • The air-conditioned Sonora becomes the Bud Light Backyard.
  • Gobi becomes the Yellowstone Dutton Ranch.
  • The craft beer barn is renamed for Guy Fieri.84

The main stage turns too. Stagecoach's Mane Stage is set 90 degrees clockwise from where Coachella's biggest stage stood.84 In 2014, Rat Sound's crew described the main stage moving to the old Outdoor Theatre site, with half the Sahara's gear taken out.89 The Sahara is not part of Stagecoach, though you can see it in the distance. Most of the big art leaves, replaced by a horse sculpture and a cowboy boot. The green grass is mostly gone by then. Stagecoach allows lawn chairs; Coachella does not.84

The smaller festival is not the smaller draw. In 2024 Indio counted 89,003 Stagecoach tickets, against 81,690 for Coachella's first weekend and 80,148 for its second.55 Stagecoach also gets the end of the season's wind: on April 25, 2026, with gusts of 50-65 mph forecast, it was paused and evacuated for about an hour and a half.90

The crew window covers all of it. AEG's 2025 stagehand posting asked for availability from March 17 to May 9, with the expected work running March 30 to May 3.91 Five weeks, three weekends, one patch of grass.

Still open

  • When the 2012 land purchase closed, and for how much. Riverside County's deed records should say.
  • A current, official acreage figure, from Goldenvoice or Indio's planning department.
  • Why drinks were fenced in for 23 years: license condition, permit term or policy?
  • Whether Spectra stands all year, and which numbered polo fields sit under which stages.

Chapter 04

04The polo club

The short versionYou pay a lot of money every year to hang out at a polo club, and the polo club stopped playing polo. It ended in November 2021, when Goldenvoice took over Empire year-round and staff got three days' notice. Desert polo lives on next door at Eldorado, on land Goldenvoice owns and leases back until the festival needs it. Here is how the game works, what the club was, and what the grounds do now when nobody is riding on them.

You pay a lot of money every year to hang out at a polo club. In 2026, a general admission pass for the first weekend cost $649.92 And the polo club stopped playing polo.

On October 27, 2021, Goldenvoice announced it was taking over Empire Polo Club year-round.14 Some of the club's staff had been told the day before that they were being laid off, with three days' notice.73 "These people gave over 30 years of their employment - of their life," one of them told KESQ.73 By November it was official: polo would stop at Empire.93

Palm Springs Life now puts it flatly: polo is no longer played at Empire Polo Club.9 The name stayed. The horses moved next door.

This chapter explains the game you are standing on, the club that played it, and what the grounds do for the rest of the year.

How polo works

Polo is hockey on horseback with a safety system built around a single imaginary line. Here is enough to follow a match, or to play the game at the end of this chapter.

The field

An outdoor polo field is 300 by 160 yards, about nine American football fields.94 The goals sit at each end, 8 yards apart, with no crossbar, so a ball that goes between the posts at any height counts.9596 Low sideboards, no taller than 11 inches, are optional and keep the ball in play along the sides.95

A goal counts no matter who puts the ball through, including a pony.95 After every goal the teams switch ends, which evens out the wind and the turf.9495

Four players, four jobs

Outdoor polo is four a side.95 The positions are numbered on the jerseys:9796

  • No. 1 is the most forward attacker and the main scorer. It is often the team's least experienced player, whose job includes marking the other side's No. 4.
  • No. 2 is the second attacker, the one who fights hardest for the ball.
  • No. 3 is the pivot, usually the best player on the team and often the captain. It turns defense into attack and feeds Nos. 1 and 2.
  • No. 4, the back, defends the goal and sends the ball forward.

Chukkers

A match is divided into periods called chukkers. Under the US Polo Association's (USPA) outdoor rules, a horn sounds after seven minutes of play, and up to 30 seconds of overtime follow. The chukker ends at the next horn, wherever the ball is.98 US matches usually run six chukkers outdoors, with four to six depending on the tournament.94 Argentina plays eight in its biggest opens; four is common in England and Europe.96

A tie is not a tie for long. Play continues after the last chukker "until one side obtains a goal."98

Handicaps

Every player has a rating in "goals," from -2 up to 10, with 10 the best. It is not a count of goals scored. It measures horsemanship, hitting, game sense, the quality of the player's ponies and sportsmanship, and the USPA reviews it twice a year.94

A team's handicap is the sum of its four players' ratings. Tournaments are capped by that total, so a "12-goal" tournament means no team can add up to more than 12.95

In a handicap match, the weaker team starts with goals already on the board. The formula: take the difference in team ratings, divide by six, and multiply by the number of chukkers. In a six-chukker game, a 4-goal team playing a 6-goal team starts 2-0 up.9495 It is one of the few sports that tries to make an unequal game fair before it starts.

The line of the ball

This is the rule that makes polo polo. At any moment, the ball's path through the air or across the grass extends into an imaginary line, forwards and backwards. That is the line of the ball.97

The right of way belongs to the player following that line most closely, at the smallest angle to it, with the ball on their right.9997 Nobody may cross in front of that player unless they are far enough ahead that there is no chance of a collision.99 Crossing is the foul you will see called most; the USPA says right-of-way and ride-off violations are the most frequent calls.97

You can't cut across the line, but you can push your way onto it:

  • The ride-off. Two riders go side by side and shove with horse and body to move each other off the line. It is legal only at a shallow angle; the Polo Museum puts the limit at 45 degrees.95 Two teammates may not ride off one opponent at the same time.99
  • The hook. A player can block an opponent's swing with their own mallet, but only from the same side as the swing or directly behind it, only while the opponent is hitting, and never above shoulder height.9997

Right hand only

Every player must hold the mallet in the right hand. The USPA exempts only left-handers registered before January 1, 1974.9594 The reason is safety. Riders often meet head-on at a gallop, and if everyone swings on the same side, the swings and the paths stay predictable.100 The right-of-way rules assume that swing, which is why the ball should be on your right.99

Left-handed play has been banned before. According to the Dawson & Bell Polo Club, it was barred in the mid-1930s, allowed again after the Second World War when players were scarce, and banned again by the USPA in 1974.100

The mallet is a bamboo cane, 48-54 inches long, with a cigar-shaped head. Players hit with the side of the head, not the end.95 The outdoor ball is hard plastic, about 3.5 inches across and 4.5 ounces, and it travels roughly three times faster than the rider who hit it.95

The ponies

They are called ponies by tradition. Most stand well over pony height and most are Thoroughbreds or crosses.95 The pony is said to account for "50% to 80%" of a player's ability.95

A pony covers one to two miles in a chukker, so players change horses between chukkers. Every player needs at least two horses for a match, and high-goal players bring a whole string and often use a fresh horse every chukker.95

Penalties

Two mounted umpires call fouls. If they disagree, a referee at midfield decides.95 Penalties are numbered, and the worse the foul, the closer to goal the free hit:9799

  • Penalty 1: a goal, awarded outright, for a dangerous or deliberate foul that stops a likely score.
  • Penalty 2: a free hit from 30 yards, or from the spot of the foul.
  • Penalty 3: a free hit from 40 yards, in front of goal.
  • Penalty 4: a free hit from 60 yards, in front of goal.
  • Penalty 5: a free hit from the spot of the foul or from midfield.
  • Penalty 6, the "safety": a 60-yard hit for the attackers when a defender knocks the ball over their own back line.

Penalties 7 to 10 cover the procedural end: re-taking botched hits, sending off a pony or a player, and ejection.97101

Divot stomping

At halftime, the spectators walk onto the field and tread the clods of turf the hooves have kicked up back into place. It is a social tradition, sometimes with champagne. The USPA advises wedges, not heels.94

It is also, if you squint, the only part of polo that resembles Coachella: a few thousand well-dressed people walking around on a polo field, looking at the grass.

Empire's polo years

Al Haagen III founded Empire Polo Club in 1987, four years after he took up the sport.10 The Polo Museum says his name is "nearly synonymous with polo in California."10 The club hosted the first 40-goal event in the US and the first US Women's Open, ran a polo school, fielded at least 15 teams each winter, and drew what the museum calls "record-setting attendance" on Sundays.10 Around 2015, Palm Springs Life put the Sunday crowds at more than 4,000, mostly free of charge, over a 13-week season.102

It also had something no other US club had: the only fully lit grass polo field in the country, used for Friday "Polo Under the Lights."64 Palm Springs Life went further and called it one of only two in the world.102

The grass was the whole point. In 2015 the club watered its fields 11 months a year with groundwater and Colorado River canal water, through computer-controlled sprinklers, and Goldenvoice managed April.13 "Polo ponies run on grass. They don't run on dirt," Haagen told The Desert Sun.13

The lore · Hollywood on horseback

The legend goes that the valley's polo goes back to the movie stars. Palm Springs Life tells it this way: Hollywood had taken up polo by the 1920s, Walt Disney led the Palm Springs horsemen, and Spencer Tracy and Clark Gable "likely" rode the early makeshift fields.102 That "likely" is the magazine's own. The documented history of the clubs starts later, with Eldorado in 1957.

Empire's biggest polo moment never happened. It was chosen to host the XII FIP World Polo Championship in October 2021.103 Covid postponed it, and when it was played, from October 26 to November 6, 2022, it was in Florida, with the final at Wellington.104 By then there was no polo at Empire to go back to.

Eldorado, next door

Desert polo did not end in 2021. It moved over the fence.

Eldorado Polo Club is the older of the two. It was founded in 1957 as a members' club at the original Eldorado Country Club in Indian Wells, and rising land values pushed it to Indio by 1979. It was the valley's top polo facility until Empire opened next door.102 In the 1990s it hosted 22-goal polo and the US Open.105

In 2012 Goldenvoice bought it, along with the farms around it.59 The sale "included a leaseback that let Eldorado keep hosting polo."106 The club now plays on 13 fields, plus two at Empire's Calhoun Ranch and one at a private ranch. Goldenvoice owns the land, and Eldorado leases it until April, when Goldenvoice hosts Coachella and Stagecoach.105

That makes the polo players the festival's tenants, on land that used to be theirs, in a season that ends where the festival's build begins.

The season itself runs roughly mid-December to late March. Eldorado's 2018 tournaments ran from January 6 to April 1.106 In 2024-25 it had practices from mid-December, women's tournaments between Christmas and New Year, and four 12-goal tournaments in February and March.105

The overlap is real. The festival has been spreading onto Eldorado land for years: The Desert Sun described "the adjacent Eldorado Polo Club, also used by the festival" in 2015,13 and the Sahara moved onto it in 2018.83 For 2024, the footings for the new Sahara went in starting in December, while polo season was getting under way next door.83

What stays now

Year-round control means Goldenvoice can build things that don't have to come down.

  • The Radiohead bunker. In September 2025 Goldenvoice applied to Indio for a permit for a 16,130-square-foot "commercial foundation," valued at $2.5 million.107 It became an underground cinema for Radiohead's KID A MNESIA: 17,000 square feet and 38 feet high, beneath the polo fields. Loose desert sand ruled out piles, so the builders dug out the whole footprint, built a concrete box with a steel frame, and buried it. The roof carries sand, turf and up to 1,000 people standing on top. Its designers, Wonder Works, say it "remains on the Coachella site as a permanent fixture."108 The Desert Sun was less sure what becomes of it next year.109 The exhibition was announced to travel on to Brooklyn, Chicago, Mexico City and San Francisco; the bunker stayed in Indio.110
  • The steel Sahara. The 2024 rebuild swapped aluminum for steel, on about 1,500 tons of concrete, roughly 156 truckloads, poured overnight to keep the trucks off Indio's streets.83 The tent's footing has been permanent since at least 2018, buried two feet under the grass.82 Whether the steel itself stays up between festivals, no source says.
  • The Rose Garden. Three acres, about 8,000 rose bushes, three Victorian gazebos and a 5,000-square-foot pavilion. It was the edge of the Sunday polo field; now it is the festival's VIP garden and a wedding venue.111
  • The Forum. A clear-span event building of 48,000 square feet, with a main structure 300 by 160 feet and a 60-foot ceiling.112 That is a polo field's proportions, in feet instead of yards. It is probably a coincidence.
  • Hacienda del Toro. Three acres with a 16,000-square-foot permanent tent.57
  • Medjhool Lake. Ten acres with a lake and three tents.57 It is named for the dates grown on the property, and its walkways and footbridges are modeled on Monet's garden at Giverny.9

Some festival art now stays permanently, though not on the grounds. Francis Kéré's Sarbalé ke stands in Dr. Carreon Park in Indio, and HANNAH's Monarchs at Lake Cahuilla Veterans Regional Park.113

The rest of the year

The 2021 deal opened the club to "special events, sporting events, and private functions."14 Here is what that looks like.

Other festivals. The 2013 Indio deal allowed up to five festivals a year on the site.66 The first big extra was Desert Trip in October 2016: the Rolling Stones, Bob Dylan, Paul McCartney, Neil Young, Roger Waters and the Who, over two weekends, with seats up to $1,599 and 1,000 flushable toilets, against the usual 300.114 Tollett told The New York Times it grossed "approximately $160 million for the two weekends."115 Stereogum called it "Oldchella."114 Power Trip followed on October 6-8, 2023, with Iron Maiden, Guns N' Roses, Judas Priest, AC/DC, Tool and Metallica, for up to 80,000 a day. Judas Priest replaced Ozzy Osbourne, who cancelled.116

Soccer. Goldenvoice said after the takeover that it wanted other sports on the fields, naming lacrosse, women's field hockey and soccer.9 Soccer arrived. AEG's Coachella Valley Invitational, a preseason tournament for MLS and NWSL clubs, has run there since 2022. The 2026 edition ran February 7-14 for MLS and February 15-21 for NWSL, with 21 clubs.117 The Desert Magazine describes two match fields divided by bleachers, a practice field, and a white picket fence, with the turf grown about 15 miles away on the farm that supplies the LA Galaxy's stadium.118 "Quality turf is really important to the game of soccer," AEG's Kara Korber told the magazine.118 The headline put it best: "Forget polo."

Dogs, lacrosse and lights. The grounds' public calendar includes the Kennel Club of Palm Springs Dog Show (January 8-11, 2027), the Sand Storm Lacrosse Festival in its 20th year with more than 450 teams (January 16-17, 2027), the Southwest Arts Festival, a Veterans Day 5K and 10K, the Magic of Lights drive-through in November and December, and the City of Indio's July 4 fireworks and drone show.119

Weddings and the rest. The Rose Garden and the other venues are rented for weddings, corporate meetings, product launches and trade shows. Team-building packages include golf cart polo, hot air balloons, and "live exhibition polo matches."5763 AEG's sales page pitches groups from 15 to 90,000.63

So polo is still available at Empire Polo Club, as a corporate add-on.

What might come next. In June 2025 the owner of Empire Polo Club proposed a mixed-use "lifestyle hub" of about 218 acres next to the festival grounds, with housing, retail, dining and possibly a sports venue. County officials approved a key land-use change; final approval was pending.120

Still open

  • Which numbered polo fields sit under which Coachella stages.
  • What it costs to repair the grass after three festival weekends. The only public line is that the grass is "restored after the festivals."13
  • Whether Spectra and the Sahara steel stand all year.

You have stood on the field. Now ride it. Try it: /play/

Chapter 05

05The stages and the spaces within

The short versionCoachella opened in 1999 with a main stage, a second stage and three tents named for deserts, and it has been adding rooms ever since. The Sahara has moved three times and now stands on 1,500 tons of concrete. Do LaB started as a broken water feature and grew into a stage with its own secret lineup and its own festival. Then come the nightclub tent, the punk tent, the sound system with seven stacks, a dome, a beer house and a brand-new stage built in under four months.

Coachella names its tents after deserts, and nobody on record has said why. Mojave, Gobi and Sahara were there in October 1999, three "rave tents" beside a main stage and a second stage.121 Yuma joined in 2013 and Sonora in 2017.122123 Since then the festival has added a dance room with no desert in its name, a stage for three-hour sets, a dome, a beer house and a stage that books itself.

The lore · The desert names

The festival's tent names make a tidy set: Sahara, Mojave, Gobi, Yuma, Sonora. Every guide repeats that they're named for deserts, and Wikipedia confirms the desert sources for the first four.18 What nobody has published is the meeting where someone said "let's name them after deserts." No interview with Paul Tollett or anyone at Goldenvoice on the subject turned up in this research. Two later rooms seem to break the rule. Quasar does. The Antarctic, a projection dome from 2017, may not: by Britannica's count, the largest desert on Earth is the Antarctic Ice Sheet.124 Whether whoever named the dome knew that is another thing nobody has said. If you know who named the Sahara, the corrections address is in the footer.

Three tents and a polo field

The 1999 Sahara was a dance tent from the first day. Its opening bill included A-Trak, Juan Atkins, Kevin Saunderson and Richie Hawtin, and it was pushed to the far edge of the grounds, beside what later became the Rose Garden VIP area, with only a few light panels.82 That first weekend was hot enough that Kid Koala's records started to warp on the turntables.3

The two open-air stages are a smaller mystery. Palm Springs Life's account of 1999 lists a "main stage" and a "second stage."121 Wikipedia says the Outdoor Theatre has been in use since the start, next to the main stage, and its lineup tables use the name from early on without saying when it was first used.18 So the second stage of 1999 is probably the Outdoor Theatre under a plainer name, but nothing found here says so outright.

The main stage and the Outdoor Theatre

The main stage, now the Coachella Stage, is the one on the posters and the livestream thumbnails. In 2013 it was built by Brown United on six legs, and the stagehands called it "Optimus Brown." It carried about 165,000 pounds of gear on a structure rated for 500,000.125 Rat Sound has handled the audio almost from the start - 18 of the first 19 years, as of 2018 - and that year the main stage field alone had 168 L-Acoustics K2 cabinets.126

The Outdoor Theatre sits next to it, and it is the stage people mean when they say "sunset set."127 In 2013 it had a 65 x 45 ft roof and sliding trusses so one act's lights could roll off while the next act's rolled on.125 It also has the strangest second life of any stage on the grounds. When the country festival Stagecoach takes over the polo fields after Coachella, its main stage goes up on the Outdoor Theatre site, according to a 2014 report.89

The Sahara keeps moving

The Sahara is the tent that changed the festival, and it has spent most of its life being moved around the grounds.

In 2002 Coachella expanded the Sahara's production and bookings, with Hawtin, Moby, Paul Oakenfold, Tiësto and Pete Tong.128 In 2006 it held the show the next chapter is about, plus a surprise Madonna set the following day; fans now date the American dance boom to that weekend.82 In 2011 Odd Future played one of the tent's first live, non-DJ sets.82

By 2013 the Sahara was 80 ft tall.83 PLSN described it as a hangar holding about 20,000 people, built and lit by Andrew Gumper's AG Lighting and Sound.125 Then it started to travel:

  • Until 2018: near the Rose Garden at Empire Polo Club, its home for years.83
  • 2018: onto the newly bought Eldorado Polo Club land next door, by the entrance, built 25% bigger.83 The LA Times gives the result as nearly 100 ft tall with a 250-ft stage, rated for 90 mph winds, and anchored to permanent concrete footings about two feet under the grass.82
  • 2024: south again, to land between Avenue 51 and Polo Road that Goldenvoice's Jason Brown called a "whole new area of the venue that we've never been into before."83

The 2024 version is not a tent in any camping sense. The old frame was aluminum; the new one is steel, which Brown said allows "more weight capacity and production elements." Work started in December. Crews poured about 156 truckloads of concrete, roughly 1,500 tons, into footings underground, and worked overnight so the trucks would not clog Indio's streets.83

How big is it? The Desert Sun gives 320 ft long.83 Elation, the lighting maker that supplied the rig, gives 400 x 410 ft, nearly 100 ft tall, on 16 steel ribs and 40 ft wider than the old one.129 The two may be measuring different things. Either way, it hung 956 Elation fixtures, designed by Todd Roberts of Production Design Works, and ran four headliner-level acts a day with changeovers as short as 10 to 15 minutes.129

The move came with a new grass hill and a "Sahara Express Walkway," and some fans said the walk from the main stage took about 15 minutes.130 The tent's old ground did not stay empty. That is where Quasar went.131

Mojave and Gobi

The two other 1999 tents are the quieter siblings. The Mojave is the second-largest tent and books indie rock and rising acts; the Gobi is the smallest of the classic tents, shaded and rarely packed.127 In 2013 the Mojave hung motion-controlled "noodles" - long tubes that moved and doubled as a 3D projection surface - from a 60 x 40 ft truss grid, while the Gobi used a 60 x 40 ft Versa Stage with a roof.125

According to Wikipedia, both moved behind an access road in 2017, and in 2018 the Mojave was enlarged and moved into the Sahara's old spot near the Rose Garden.18 From 2015 they also started booking more electronic acts, as dance music spread out of the Sahara.128

Every spring the tents also change jobs. When Stagecoach takes over the grounds, the Mojave becomes the Palomino, Yuma becomes Diplo's Honkytonk, Sonora becomes the air-conditioned Bud Light Backyard, and the Gobi becomes the Yellowstone Dutton Ranch.84

Yuma: a nightclub in a tent

Yuma opened in 2013 as the sixth stage, in the terrace food area near the Heineken Dome.125 Dancing Astronaut called it "the mysterious new Yuma Tent" and found an indoor nightclub inside: wood floors, disco balls, fake Baroque portraits, couches and air conditioning. Four Tet opened it with a 90-minute improvised set, followed by DJ Harvey, Seth Troxler and Luciano.122

The idea, per Wikipedia, was a room that "dials down the noise and strobe lights" for underground acts.18 Its longtime lighting designer, Steve Lieberman, kept it that way: lights only, no video wall, dark and minimal.132 In a festival built around LED screens the size of buildings, the room's selling point is that you can barely see.

Sonora: the punk tent

Sonora arrived in 2017, not 2016 or 2018 as some lists say. LAist announced it with the headline "New stage, who dis?" and a rock-leaning bill with a "bilingual slant": Allah-Las, T.S.O.L., Guided By Voices and Downtown Boys.123 It is fully enclosed and club-sized, and its booker, Rene Contreras, put the brief simply.133

"We just wanted to have an intimate space for guitar bands." - Rene Contreras133

It has since booked rock, hardcore, early hip-hop and Latin acts, from Los Mirlos to The Linda Lindas, Viagra Boys, PUP and Los Bitchos.133

Do LaB: the stage that let itself in

Do LaB is the best story on the grounds, because nobody planned it.

It is run by three brothers, Dede, Josh and Jesse Flemming; Josh and Jesse are twins. Jesse books the music, Josh designs the structures and Dede runs production and deals with Goldenvoice.134 They started with renegade parties in the late 1990s, going back to a birthday party in the mountains in 1999.135136 A downtown LA warehouse they ran was shut down by police and fire officials, which Josh says is what made them go legal.137

The broken water feature

Sources argue about the first year. Variety and NPR say 2004; the brothers' own accounts to The FADER, Pollstar and Entertainment Weekly say 2005, when a friend who curated art for the festival invited them to build something.138137139136 The likeliest reading is that 2004 is when the company and its own festival began, and 2005 is when it first came to Coachella.

What they built in 2005 was a dome with a water feature that did not work very well.139

"Everybody was showering in it and drinking from it." - Josh Flemming139

The wind mangled everything they set up on the first day.139 The budget was tiny; in 107-degree heat they misted people and handed out orange slices.136 Then, around midnight, the main music shut off. The brothers kept playing, and the crowd walked over - roughly a thousand people, by EW's account.139135 Jesse says that was the first time they saw it "could be more than an installation."139

In 2006 Paul Tollett overbooked two DJs and parked them in the Do LaB dome, with extra sound and lights. Dede called it "the green light for us to go for it and actually play music."139

An inch, then a mile

From there it grew, one negotiation at a time. Dede describes 2008 as the year they went to 360-degree environments: "they give us an inch, we take a mile."139 In 2009 Tollett asked them to take their sign down, because Coachella had no branding on site at all; they talked him into keeping it, which made it one of the first branded things at the festival.139 By 2010 a performance stage was pushing it toward "more of a stage and less of an art installation."139 In 2011 Josh's Calatrava-inspired "fish fry" structure was, in his words, their lowest point; it "barely stood up."139

In 2014 it moved to a corner with a backstage, and the brothers started calling it "the official sixth stage at Coachella."139 The festival still does not put Do LaB on the set-time grid. It lists it on the poster, in small type, as an art installation, and the lineup comes out separately on Do LaB's own channels, usually in late March.138 Dede called the tiny poster credit a "badge of honor."135

Water stayed the theme. Misters, water cannons and performers with flower-covered water guns have been cooling the crowd since the start; as Dede put it, "we knew how intense it was out here."134137 By 2022 the structure was a 200 x 200 ft tent of colored fabric triangles holding about 10,000 people at night.137 In 2025 the stage ran on battery power, according to the festival.140

The surprise sets

Do LaB books itself. Jesse runs it apart from the festival-wide booking, and the aim is to "catch artists right before they're about to pop."134 Dede: "We're not trying to sell tickets."138 Closing slots are sometimes booked hours before they start.138

The surprises began, Jesse says, because "we left it open to see what would happen."141 The first big one was Bonobo in 2015, the first major artist from outside their circle of friends, and the reaction made them leave a couple of open slots every year after.139 Since then:

  • 2017: Kaskade was booked as the surprise and tweeted about it. Security said the area could not hold the crowd that would follow, so the slot went to Skrillex instead.139
  • 2018: FISHER and Chris Lake drew the biggest afternoon crowds Do LaB had seen, and both credit those sets with lifting their careers.139
  • 2019: Major Lazer, with Diplo.142
  • 2022: Diplo again, plus Rebecca Black and MEUTE.137
  • 2024: Billie Eilish, two years after she headlined Coachella.143 Josh's estimate: "It must've been over 20,000 people," the biggest crowd the stage has had. Anderson .Paak played a surprise set as DJ Pee .Wee the same year.139

The run has had one bad night. On Friday, April 10, 2026, Do LaB closed after a light reportedly fell on a woman during a surprise John Summit set, and later sets were cancelled; at the time Mixmag reported it, details came from attendees and there was no official statement.144

Lightning in a Bottle

Do LaB also runs its own festival. Lightning in a Bottle took its name from a friend's private July birthday party in the early 2000s, and Wikipedia's history dates the first event under that name to 2004 in the Angeles National Forest.145 Other outlets count from 2006, the first three-day public edition.135146 It moved around California for years, and per Wikipedia has been at Buena Vista Lake in Kern County since 2019.145

The two festivals run back to back. The brothers start building Lightning in a Bottle about five days after Coachella's second weekend.136 In 2024 Jesse told Pollstar the independent festival was up against Live Nation, AEG and their radius clauses - the contract terms that stop artists playing nearby festivals.136 It is still running: the 2026 edition was set for May 20-24 at Buena Vista Lake, with Empire of the Sun, Zeds Dead and Chase & Status.147

Quasar and the Red Bull Mirage

Quasar is the newest stage, and it exists because 60 to 90 minutes is not long enough for some DJs. Goldenvoice announced it in March 2024 as a stage for sets of three hours or more, on both weekends.148 Its designer, Heather Shaw of Vita Motus - who also helped design Do LaB and has been to every Coachella since 1999 - links the idea to 2023, when Fred again.., Skrillex and Four Tet stepped in for Frank Ocean, and to the problem that 60 to 90 minutes is too short for a full arc.149

Shaw heard about the project at the end of January 2024, which gave her under four months to build it, the tightest timeline of her career.149 The result is two asymmetric LED walls showing a live Unreal Engine model of the Coachella landscape, rendered in real time.149 It sits on the Sahara's former ground.131 The 2024 bookings set the format: Honey Dijon with Green Velvet, a Jamie xx, Floating Points and Daphni b3b, and a sunset set from Eric Prydz b2b Anyma.148131

In 2025 Red Bull built a place to watch it from. KTLA described the Red Bull Mirage as a two-story, 20,000-square-foot installation near Quasar, with bars and shade, open to any GA wristband.150 By 2026 Flaunt was calling it three tiers, with DJ sets, a daytime happy hour and a Nobu omakase dinner on the Saturday; the top level was mostly kept for artists and guests.151 Whether it grew a floor between years, or the two outlets counted differently, is not clear.

Despacio: seven stacks and one disco ball

Despacio is a sound system, not a stage, and it is the opposite of everything else at the festival. James Murphy of LCD Soundsystem and David and Stephen Dewaele of Soulwax built it with studio designer John Klett, who dates the first conversations to around May 2010.152 It made its debut at the Manchester International Festival in July 2013: three vinyl-only nights, seven 11-ft speaker stacks around the room, and nearly 50,000 watts.153 It is vinyl only because, its makers say, the system "would definitely notice" anything else.152

It came to Coachella in 2016, in its own tent, with the stacks arranged around a circular lighting rig and a big disco ball. On April 22, the day after Prince died, they played "Purple Rain."154

It came back in 2023, open 3 to 9 p.m. both weekends, with 44 McIntosh amplifiers, about 950 people on the floor, a target of 105 dB, and the disco ball as the only visual.155

The Antarctic

The one room named for a cold place was a dome. In 2017 Goldenvoice put up a 120-ft geodesic projection dome, billed as the largest in the world, built by Obscura with Pacific Domes. It had 15 Christie projectors, 108 L-Acoustics speakers in three tiers and an eight-minute, 360-degree program called "Chrysalis," for 500 people at a time.156 In 2019 the air-conditioned dome came back as YouTube's 360 dance dome, and the Antarctic program returned with it.157

Heineken House

Heineken has been Coachella's official beer since 2002.7677 Before the House there were the Heineken Domes; PLSN used one as a landmark in 2013.125 In 2014 Heineken House replaced them, with sets from Skrillex, Outkast, Lorde and Girl Talk, Snapchat clues to secret performers, and beer lockers opened by fingerprint.76 In 2019 it moved, got a new structure and its first 21-plus beer garden, and booked De La Soul and The Roots.77 By 2025 it was air-conditioned, with Pusha T and Afrojack.158

Retired, temporary and never built

Some rooms did not last.

  • The Oasis Dome: an open-air dome "built into a makeshift tree," with 360-degree sound; Breakage played it in 2011.159 Pacific Domes describes a 44-ft dome with misters overhead.160
  • The Heineken Domes: replaced by Heineken House in 2014.76
  • Despacio and the Antarctic: pop-ups that come back when someone wants them to, so far in 2016 and 2023, and 2017 and 2019.154156157
  • The Kanye dome: for 2019, Kanye West reportedly wanted a huge dome in the middle of the grounds. Goldenvoice said no, partly because it would have meant moving stages and a toilet area tied to sewer and water lines, and Ariana Grande took the slot within 24 hours. That account comes from Billboard's reporting, via Digital Music News; neither side confirmed it.161

Still open

  • Who named the tents after deserts, and when the second stage became the Outdoor Theatre.
  • The 2024 Sahara's real footprint: 320 ft long, or 400 x 410 ft.
  • Whether the Red Bull Mirage gained a third level in 2026 or was always that tall.

Chapter 06

06Daft Punk and the pyramid

The short versionIn 2006 Daft Punk said yes to Coachella after a year of saying no, because their manager, who usually turned offers down without asking them, picked up the phone. They played the Sahara tent at 11 p.m. on a Saturday, inside a pyramid of LED blocks built for that one show, and ran nearly 20 minutes past curfew, with the promoter promising to cover any fine. The pyramid went on to a world tour, a live album, two Grammys and, 20 years later, a LEGO set. The crowd count and the bands it started are legends; the rest is on the record.

The pyramid was built for one show. Live Design, the trade magazine that covered it, put it plainly: the set "was first designed for a one-time performance" at Coachella.162 It ended up on a 48-date world tour, a Grammy-winning live album and, announced in 2026, a LEGO set.163164165 All of that started with a phone call their manager almost didn't make.

The offer

Coachella asked twice. According to the 2015 documentary Daft Punk Unchained, the festival offered $250K in 2005 and was turned down, then came back in 2006 with $300K, and the band said yes.166

Goldenvoice tells it with a bigger number. In its own 2024 film, Stacey Vee, Goldenvoice's VP of festival talent, says the 2006 offer was $350K, plus airfare, hotel and ground transportation.167 As far as this research found, it is the first figure the promoter itself has put on it. The two may be counting differently - a fee against a full package - or one of them is wrong. Both are on camera.

"We really didn't want a no; we really wanted this one to happen." - Stacey Vee, Goldenvoice167

The yes is the part that almost didn't happen. Pedro Winter, known as Busy P, managed Daft Punk for 12 years, starting at 20, and founded the label Ed Banger along the way. He describes the job mostly as refusal.167

"My job mostly during those 12 years was to say no to everybody, everything." - Pedro Winter167

Usually he said no without even asking the band.168 The 2006 offer arrived as a short email of a few lines, and this time, he says, he at least took the trouble to call Thomas Bangalter.168 Bangalter said yes. The plan grew quickly into a pyramid made of LEDs.168

Bangalter's own version, from an August 2007 interview that stayed unpublished until 2021, is about money in the useful sense.

"Coachella was a big offer financially, and that triggered the ability to bring the show to the next level." - Thomas Bangalter169

He added that they had "never done anything for the money."169 Water & Music reports the band put advance money into the production, trading profit for a better show.170 It would be their first US show since 1997, and the first time anyone heard Discovery played live.170171

By the numbers · The deal

  • 2005 offer: $250K, declined (Unchained)166
  • 2006 offer: $300K (Unchained)166 or $350K plus flights, hotel and ground transport (Goldenvoice)167
  • Last US show before it: 1997170
  • Set length: about 75 minutes172
  • Over curfew: nearly 20 minutes172
  • Pyramid blocks: more than 1,600 Barco O-Lites173
  • Production cost: not documented anywhere found

The film: Busy P Says "Oui"

The best first-hand account of the booking is a short film the festival made about itself. Busy P Says "Oui" - Ed Banger, Daft Punk & Coachella premiered on Coachella's YouTube channel on December 18, 2024.167174 It runs about ten minutes, was directed by Joey Garfield, and was shot at and around Coachella 2024 as part of Goldenvoice's own content slate.175167

It interviews Winter, Vee, and Wiley Dailey, whom Billboard calls the mastermind of the Sahara tent, and it includes footage of the 2006 set that had not been seen before, alongside Justice's later Coachella shows.167175 Two lines carry it.

"Sometimes a 'Oui' can change your life." - Pedro Winter175

"People showed up and lost their minds." - Wiley Dailey167

It is a promoter's film about its own best night, and it plays like one. It is also where the $350K comes from, and the source for the phone call.167168 (YouTube would not load for this research, so the details here come from Billboard, Radio FG and BPM's coverage of the film, not from a transcript.)

It is one of three films that cover the night. Daft Punk Unchained, directed by Hervé Martin-Delpierre, premiered on Showtime in the US on December 10, 2015, and its Coachella segment is where the $250K-then-$300K story, the locked-out manager and the 40,000 figure all come from.176166177 The festival's own Coachella: 20 Years in the Desert, a 104-minute film by Chris Perkel, went up on YouTube on April 10, 2020, and its director said it included footage of the 2006 set that had never been shown publicly.178 Fans have their own: crowd footage that the uploader says came from an old file shared on Reddit went up on YouTube in 2025, as reported by attendees.179

Building the pyramid

The man who designed it was Martin Phillips of Bionic League, the lighting and set designer, who also co-produced the video and programmed the show.162180 He based it on the video for "Technologic," which he had also designed.170 The duo were closely involved. "Simplicity was a key element for them," he said.180

What he built, by the 2006 trade accounts:

  • The pyramid itself: more than 1,600 Barco O-Lite LED blocks, pixel-mapped into one 3D video surface. Video ran over the bottom two-thirds of the front and a small section above the platform where the duo stood.173 PLSN's 2007 gear list gives 1,800 modules, probably for the touring rig.180 Rolling Stone Australia and The Ringer both call it a 24-foot aluminum pyramid.181171
  • The sides: two 18 x 18 ft frames of criss-crossing hexagons, made from 170 Element Labs VersaTubes.173
  • The back: a pixel curtain 48 ft wide and 16 ft high, built for the show by Element Labs and Daft Arts.173
  • The build: VER Los Angeles made the custom two-faced pyramid screens, with a "top-hat" peak, from Barco templates; XL Video ran video; B&R Scenic later rebuilt the frame to survive the road.180
  • The brains: Catalyst media servers ran about 95% of the video, triggered from a Hog console by timecode sent from the band's own rig. Phillips kept three layers free for "buskable" improvised content.162180

Inside the structure, Bangalter said in 2007, "We have synthesizers and remote controls in the pyramid."169 The look on the surface was the 1970s and 1980s - blocky color, then wireframe graphics, then photographs - on hardware that was new that year.180

None of the parts were invented for it. That was the point, Phillips said.

"What was new was the fact that [they] had taken the existing products and used them as building blocks." - Martin Phillips170
