Because production time was short, he "locked them into a set shell" - a fixed beginning, middle and end - so the music cues could fire the lights and video, while the music inside the shell was improvised in Ableton Live.182170 On the sound side, he recalled, "it was really a case of a guy on the console trying to keep up."170

What it cost is the missing number in this chapter. No documented figure turned up.

The people behind the robots

A pyramid that runs on timecode needs someone ready for the night the timecode stops. On the tour that was Eric Belanger, the lighting director, who was set up to run the whole show by hand if it failed.180 The video content came from Baptiste Andrieux and the Santa Monica studio 8VFX, and Cédric Hervet of Daft Arts cut the material to tempo and made most of the footage for the last song and the encore.180 For the encore the duo wore robot suits lit with electroluminescent wire, made by Janet Hansen of Enlighted, with a lighting-control interface built by Anders Nelson.180

Phillips has a line about the job that doubles as advice to every designer who followed him.

"You don't go home humming the lights!" - Martin Phillips170

He also had a simpler review of the work itself: "Everyone should get to work with robots."180

Rehearsal

Even their manager was kept out. Winter first heard the show days before it, as an audio-only rehearsal; he told Unchained, "I wasn't allowed to see the lights yet."166

The duo prepared for the desert in the most literal way available. In 2026 Bangalter told The Times that for the final rehearsal he had them both wear ski jackets, on the theory that "if we get through that anything will be easy."183 His review of the experience: "it was the worst ever."183

The night

Saturday, April 29, 2006. Depeche Mode headlined the main stage, and Daft Punk followed them at 11 p.m. in the Sahara tent.184170 The Sahara then sat at the far edge of the grounds, beside what later became the Rose Garden VIP area, and usually had only a few light panels.82 Why the band went there and not the main stage is not explained in anything found for this chapter.

They opened with the theme from Close Encounters of the Third Kind and went into a "Robot Rock" and "Oh Yeah" mash-up, then ran their catalog as one seamless mix.185 Setlist accounts have "Around the World" laid over "Harder, Better, Faster, Stronger," "One More Time" into "Aerodynamic," "Da Funk," and "Human After All" into "Superheroes."186 NME's report from that night says the crowd "spilled almost a hundred deep" outside the tent.187 The festival had planned for that, adding extra speaker stacks and big screens outside.177

Not everyone outside saw much. One writer, stuck by the side of the tent, recalls his housemate coming home and calling it "the best light show I've ever seen." His reply: "What light show?"177

Billboard's review put the whole weekend at more than 100,000 people over two days.184 A good share of them seemed to be trying to stand in one tent.

The set ran about 75 minutes, nearly 20 minutes past the festival's midnight curfew, which in 2006 could mean a fine.172 Paul Tollett let it run.

"They were so great we told them to keep going, and the festival would cover the possible fine." - Paul Tollett, 2006172

SPIN's live blog had it ending at midnight, with fans heading off to line up for Madonna.188 Las Vegas Weekly's account is more specific and has Tollett on the record, so this page goes with it. Madonna played the Sahara the next day too.82

Bangalter later said the crowd was "five years" behind the duo's ideas, but also that it was "the most synched-up we ever felt," in a 2013 Pitchfork interview as reported by Rolling Stone Australia.181

The lore · 40,000 into a 10,000 tent

The legend goes that 40,000 people tried to get into a tent built for 10,000. The number comes from the band's manager, in Daft Punk Unchained, and it has been repeated widely since.177189 Live Design calls it "rumored."190 Nobody counted at the gate. The Ringer goes one further and says the crowd nearly brought the tent down; no account from 2006 says so.171 What the 2006 reports do say is that the crowd stood almost a hundred deep outside, and that the festival had already put up extra speakers and screens for the people who couldn't get in.187177 However many it was, it was more than the tent.

The tour, the album, the Grammys

The pyramid was not supposed to leave Indio. It did, and XL Video had to rebuild a one-off rig for the road.162

"We've really been on a world tour since that first show at Coachella in April, 2006." - Thomas Bangalter, 2007191

Wikipedia's tour history counts 48 shows on what became Alive 2006/2007, from Coachella to Sydney Showground on December 22, 2007, the last night the pyramid was used.163 It was one of only two official tours the duo ever played; the other was in 1997.181 Along the way it reached Lollapalooza in Chicago in 2007.181

The album came from home. Alive 2007 was recorded at the Palais Omnisports de Paris-Bercy on June 14, 2007, the duo's first hometown show in ten years, and came out that November.192193 In February 2008 they made their first US TV appearance, behind Kanye West on "Stronger" at the Grammys.194 A year later they won two of their own: Best Electronic/Dance Album for Alive 2007, and Best Dance Recording for its live "Harder, Better, Faster, Stronger."164195

How much of the American dance boom that followed traces back to one tent is a matter of framing, not measurement. Fans call the 2006 weekend the "Big Bang," per the LA Times.82 The Rock and Roll Hall of Fame's library calls the set "a sea change for Electronic Dance Music."196 Water & Music traces a production "arms race" from it, through the Tupac hologram and Odesza's drones.170 The music writer Michaelangelo Matos put it more simply in Unchained: "No one had seen that level of production."189

The lore · Everyone there started a band

The legend goes that everyone who was in the Sahara that night went home and started a band, or bought decks, or both. It is the best kind of festival legend, because it cannot be checked and is too nice to want to. It also has a family tree. The original is Brian Eno's line about the Velvet Underground's first album, in the LA Times in 1982: "everyone who bought one of those 30,000 copies started a band!"197 No version naming the 2006 crowd turned up in this research, and no famous DJ is on record as having been in that tent.

The documented case is a cousin. Skrillex saw the pyramid in 2007, on the tour, not at Coachella. "I went alone, bought a ticket off a scalper for $150," he said in 2012. "It changed my life."198 Dancing Astronaut places it at the Alive 2007 show in LA.199 Ten years later he played the Do LaB stage at Coachella as a surprise act, and said he was trying to get the robots to play live again.139199

The robots and Coachella, after

Daft Punk never played Coachella again. They kept turning up anyway.200

In April 2013 the festival screened a teaser of just under two minutes for Random Access Memories on Friday, April 12: "Get Lucky," with Pharrell and Nile Rodgers, followed by a list of collaborators that included Giorgio Moroder, Julian Casablancas and Panda Bear.201202 Daft Punk weren't on the bill.201

In 2016 a site called alive2017.com counted down to October 27, and its code carried coordinates that pointed to LA and Indio. Time Out was skeptical, and nothing happened.203 In 2017 Pharrell answered the reunion rumors with the only line anyone needs: "The robots are, like... they're not repeaters."200

On February 22, 2021, the duo posted "Epilogue," an eight-minute video built from their film Electroma, and ended a 28-year partnership without giving a reason.171

Then, in October 2026, the pyramid came back as plastic. LEGO announced an official set of the Alive 2007 pyramid stage, with more details promised for 2027.165 It started as a LEGO Ideas design by Patrick Harboun, from Luxembourg, and his son, made during lockdown; it won a 2020 music challenge and later passed 10,000 supporters.165204 Cédric Hervet of Daft Arts called the pyramid "one of the most recognisable expressions of Daft Punk's visual world."165 Not bad for something built for one Saturday night.

Still open

  • What the pyramid cost, and who paid for which part.
  • Why the Sahara, and not the main stage after Depeche Mode.
  • Timestamps for Busy P Says "Oui" and the 2006 segment of Coachella: 20 Years in the Desert, if anyone has them.

Chapter 07

07The moments

The short versionThe sets people still bring up, and what the paperwork says happened. A Prince cover that kept getting pulled offline, a dead rapper reflected off a sheet of foil, a homecoming with about 200 people on stage, an ice rink that outlasted its headliner, and a $2.5M concrete bunker for a band that never showed up.

Several of Coachella's best-known moments didn't happen quite the way people remember. The Tupac on the main stage in 2012 was a projection bounced off a sheet of foil.205 Frank Ocean's ice rink was built, and the first people to stand on it were Skrillex, Fred again.. and Four Tet, a week after Ocean had left the bill.206 In 2026 the poster promised "The Bunker Debut of Radiohead," and Radiohead stayed home.107 The festival is very good at making moments. This chapter is about what was underneath them.

Daft Punk's 2006 pyramid has a chapter of its own. Everything else is here.

Prince plays "Creep" (2008)

Prince headlined the second night, April 26, 2008. Somewhere in the set he played an eight-minute cover of Radiohead's "Creep," and he brought out Sheila E. for "The Glamorous Life."207

Fan videos of "Creep" were online by the next day, and Prince's team had them taken down.207 Since the song was Radiohead's, Radiohead had a view on this. Thom Yorke's, as reported by Consequence:

"Really? He's blocked it? Well, tell him to unblock it. It's our … song."208

Seven years later Prince let it back up. In December 2015 a new upload appeared with Radiohead's blessing, and Prince tweeted a link to it.207 On December 16, 2015, it was taken down again, this time after a claim from NPG Music Publishing, which is Prince's own publishing company.208 So the Prince side won both rounds, the second one against itself.

The hologram that wasn't (2012)

On April 15, 2012, during Dr. Dre and Snoop Dogg's headline set, Tupac Shakur appeared on stage. Dre had come up with it and paid for it. "It was Dre's idea to bring Tupac back," said Nick Smith of AV Concepts, the San Diego company that projected it.205

Everybody called it a hologram. It wasn't one. It was Pepper's ghost, a 19th-century theater illusion: you project an image onto an angled transparent surface, and from the audience it looks like a person standing in the room.209 Victorian theaters did it with glass. AV Concepts used Mylar foil.205 Hypebeast named the UK firm Musion and its "Eyeliner" system as the technology underneath.209

The digital Tupac came from Digital Domain, James Cameron's visual-effects company. VFX supervisors Janelle Croshaw and Steve Preeg led a team of about 20 on it.205 Smith told MTV it took about four months.209

The lore · The $10 million Tupac

The legend goes that the Tupac cost $10 million. That figure went around in April 2012 and never came with a name attached.209 The only on-the-record number is Smith's, via MTV, and it's for a different thing: he wouldn't give the real cost, but said a comparable piece could run from $100,000 to more than $400,000.209 Nobody has published an invoice. The $10M lives on because it sounds like what a dead man on a stage ought to cost.

Then came the strange part. On September 11, 2012, five months after Coachella, Digital Domain Media Group filed for Chapter 11. It shut its operation in Port St. Lucie, Florida, let most of the 320 staff there go, and its CEO resigned.210 No source ties the bankruptcy to Tupac. It's just the order things happened in.

As for the Tupac, the digital asset "has been archived," Andscape reported in 2017, and only the estate has access.205 Dre has said it was "strictly for Coachella."205 It didn't stay strictly there for long, though. The next chapter follows it.

Beychella (2018)

Beyoncé was booked to headline in 2017. She withdrew on her doctors' advice while she was pregnant, and came back in 2018 as the first Black woman ever to headline Coachella.211 She played both Saturdays. The first was April 14.212 (GRAMMY.com gives "April 13 and 22," but those were a Friday and a Sunday.)

The show was built as an HBCU homecoming, with about 200 people on stage.211 Rehearsals ran four months for the band, then four more for the dancers.211 Fans named it Beychella.213

On YouTube, her weekend-one set peaked at about 458,000 people watching at once. That was the highest since the festival started streaming, according to TMZ's figures as reported by The FADER.212 A year later, on April 17, 2019, Netflix released Homecoming, a film of the set with a live album alongside.211 It won the Grammy for Best Music Film.214

It also raised the bar for every headliner after her. Ariana Grande's production designer told The Hollywood Reporter that after Beyoncé, the festival had turned into a "competition."215 TMZ's numbers, as The FADER reported them, had her peak about 75% above Lady Gaga's the year before, with 43.1 million live-stream views across 232 countries for the whole festival.212

Kanye, the dome and the hill (2019)

He had form. Kanye West's 2011 set came with a stone mural in the style of the Pergamon Altar, a troupe of ballerinas and a lift that was reportedly used on Michael Jackson's last tour. Complex, looking back ten years later, said nobody had ever done it like him.216

He was due to headline again in 2019. In January he walked away. He called the standard 40-by-60-foot stage "artistically limiting" and had asked, late in the day, for a custom dome. The festival didn't build it.217

He came back anyway, without a headline slot. On Easter Sunday, April 21, 2019, Sunday Service, until then an ultra-exclusive private gathering, went up on a hill at the festival at 9 a.m.218219 The "Church Clothes" merch went with it: sweatshirts from $165 to $225 and "Jesus Walks" socks at $50.220 The hill, the crowd, and the arguments over the size of the crowd all belong to the camping chapter.

Frank Ocean's rink (2023)

That year's headliners were Bad Bunny, BLACKPINK and Frank Ocean. It was the first time every headliner was nonwhite.221 Ocean closed the first Sunday, and it went wrong in several directions at once.

He came on about an hour late, in slippers.222 There was meant to be a segment with skaters on an ice rink, and it didn't happen. A source told Rolling Stone he had hurt his ankle in on-site rehearsals the week before, and the show was reworked at the last minute.222 Rolling Stone also found an LMU hockey player's Instagram Story showing nine men in skating gear, captioned "The Boys x Frank." That crew never performed.222 On stage, Ocean said he was there for his brother Ryan Breaux, who died in 2020.222

The YouTube stream was pulled. "Frank Ocean is not scheduled to appear on the Coachella livestream," YouTube posted, and nobody gave a reason. Björk's set came off the stream schedule the same weekend.223 On April 19 he withdrew from weekend two. His team said he had been hurt on the festival grounds the week before, and "on doctor's advice" couldn't perform, "due to two fractures and a sprain in his left leg."224 Blink-182, who had played their first reunion show with Tom DeLonge on the first Friday, took his slot.224225

According to Billboard, as reported by The FADER, Ocean was contracted for $8M across both weekends. His weekend-one production reportedly cost more than his $4M fee, the rink was the most expensive part of it, and the festival stood to lose "several million dollars."226 Goldenvoice went looking for someone else to use the rink. On the second Sunday, Skrillex, Fred again.. and Four Tet stood on it to close the night.206

The lore · The melted rink

Two stories went around that week. TMZ's, as Stereogum summarized it: Ocean hurt himself in an accident involving bikes on the polo grounds, doctors advised changing the set, and the skating went. Stereogum adds that about 100 LA-based hockey players had rehearsed on a raised rink at Paramount Studios and were told on arrival that the routine was off.227 The second came from TheFestiveOwl, an anonymous X account. Its version was that the rink was "constructed and ready to go," that Ocean dropped it at the last minute, and that Coachella had to "melt the entire ice rink" and rebuild the stage.227 Stereogum called the account unverified.227 The documented version is the injury his team described, and a rink that was still there a week later.224206

The bunker (2026)

The 2026 poster ended with a line nobody could explain: "The Bunker Debut of Radiohead Kid A Mnesia." Fans started guessing whether that meant Radiohead were playing.228

The permit file had the better answer. In September 2025 Goldenvoice applied to the City of Indio for a 16,130-square-foot "commercial foundation." City assessors valued it at $2.5M. Reporters found a large sand pit with steel reinforcement near the Sahara tent, and the concrete and steel framing suggested something permanent. A Rolling Stone representative confirmed Radiohead would not perform.107

What got built was Radiohead's Motion Picture House: about 17,000 square feet with 38-foot ceilings, under the Empire Polo Fields, open to anyone with a festival wristband.229 Inside were the KID A MNESIA film and galleries of full-scale Thom Yorke and Stanley Donwood artwork from the Kid A and Amnesiac sessions.229 The sound was a "6-point surround" mix built from the original multitracks, and Sean Evans directed the film.110

It had a long run-up. KID A MNESIA first came out in 2021 as a free digital exhibition for PS5, PC and Mac.110 A physical 20th-anniversary installation planned that year was cancelled over logistics and COVID.228 The desert finally got it built, five years late and underground.

So Radiohead made the 2026 poster without playing a note. Ed O'Brien did tell reporters the band plans to play 20 shows a year from 2027.110 After the festival the Motion Picture House went on tour: Brooklyn from May 6, Mexico City from October 27 and San Francisco's Palace of Fine Arts from January 14, 2027.230 The film runs 75 minutes, and visits are booked in two-hour slots. Stereogum also listed a Chicago run at Cinespace in the summer, which the official site doesn't show.230110 The official FAQ says "the Coachella Bunker experience" was different in some ways from the touring version.230 It wasn't the first artist-built world on the grounds, either. Desert Sun points to Travis Scott's "The Village by Cactus Jack" in 2025.107

Other nights people bring up

Bauhaus, upside down, 2005. It was the band's first show together since 1998. Peter Murphy opened with the nine-minute "Bela Lugosi's Dead," sung while hanging upside down over the stage like a bat.231 When it was over he told the crowd: "Now you can say that you were there!"231 The reunion bought them a tour opening for Nine Inch Nails and a 2008 album, Go Away White, and then they split again.231

Madonna and Drake, 2015. On April 12 Madonna joined Drake's headline set for "Human Nature," "Hung Up" and "Bitch I'm Madonna," then kissed him for a long time. His recoil is the clip everyone remembers.232 His Instagram caption afterward: "Moments to write home about."232

Lady Gaga, twice. In 2017 she took the headline slot Beyoncé had given up. On April 15 she debuted "The Cure" live and put it out on Spotify, iTunes and Tidal that night. It was an early case of a festival set doubling as a release.233 In 2025 she came back to open the Mayhem live show: a "demonic opera house" set, four acts and a finale, the first live "Garden of Eden," with Gesaffelstein on synths.234 BrooklynVegan's reviewer thought it was built more for the livestream and the screens than for the field.234

Ariana Grande and most of \*NSYNC, 2019. On April 14, Joey Fatone, JC Chasez, Chris Kirkpatrick and Lance Bass joined her for "Tearin' Up My Heart" and "Break Up With Your Girlfriend." She sang Justin Timberlake's parts herself.235 Nicki Minaj, Diddy and Mase turned up too. Grande told the crowd: "I've been rehearsing my whole f---ing life for this moment."235

Harry Styles and Shania Twain, 2022. On the first Friday, Styles brought out Twain for "Man! I Feel Like a Woman!" and "You're Still the One." The same set was the first live performance of "As It Was."236 His introduction: "This lady taught me to sing. She also told me that men are trash."236

Odd Future in the Sahara, 2011. On April 15, 2011, the LA collective played the Sahara tent, and it was chaos. Pharrell came out.237 The LA Times later wrote that the set "launched or boosted" Tyler, the Creator, Syd, Steve Lacy and a singer named Frank Ocean.82 Twelve years later Ocean was the one closing the main stage.

deadmau5, last call, 2025. He was one of the Sahara's early big nights, on a Friday in 2010 opposite Jay-Z.238 Fifteen years later, on April 18, 2025, his back-to-back set with ZHU was cut short while he was visibly drunk; he apologized and posted: "Probably my last Coachella."239

Justin Bieber, 2026. Bieber went from cameo regular (with Ariana Grande in 2019, with Daniel Caesar and Giveon in 2022) to Saturday headliner, on a deal he negotiated himself. Rolling Stone's Canadian edition thought the crowd might have been "the biggest ever" at Coachella, and also that "Bieber missed the mark": a minimal set heavy on nostalgia, with people leaving during the acoustic deep cuts.240

Anyma and the wind, 2026. Anyma was due to follow Sabrina Carpenter at midnight on the first Friday. The festival called it off: "Due to strong wind conditions affecting Anyma's stage build, he is unable to perform tonight."241 The same winds brought down a light fixture during John Summit's Do LaB set, injuring a festivalgoer, and that stage was closed.241 By Sunday the Do LaB had a new Instagram post: "Let's end at the beginning. Anyma b2b Marlon Hoffstadt close out the Do LaB at 10:45pm tonight."242 His full ÆDEN show moved to weekend two.241 On a site that stages a different spectacle every year, the weather got a credit.

Weekend two gets the guests (2026)

For most of the two-weekend era, weekend two was the sequel. Surprise guests were part of the house style, and the famous ones above (Madonna in 2015, \*NSYNC in 2019, Shania Twain in 2022) all came on the first weekend. In 2026 almost all of the big ones landed a week later. Madonna joined Sabrina Carpenter for three songs, timed to her new single. SZA and Sexyy Red came out for Justin Bieber, and Bieber serenaded Billie Eilish. Olivia Rodrigo joined Addison Rae, and Snoop Dogg joined Giveon.243

Variety's Jem Aswad asked the industry why. One agent said weekend one "is driven heavily by influencer culture." Another insider put it as: "Weekend two is always better... more to see and less to be seen."243 Others said guests on weekend two protect a headliner's first-weekend spotlight. Sources said it was not a plan coordinated by Goldenvoice or YouTube.243 The festival has two identical weekends on paper. In practice they've started to split: weekend one for being seen, weekend two for whoever turns up.

Still open

  • Who designed and built the bunker, what it cost beyond the $2.5M permit valuation, and whether it stays under the polo fields now that the film is on tour.
  • What the Tupac really cost. Nobody has put a figure on the record.
  • What the rink itself cost. Wikipedia credits Billboard with a figure; the reporting we could read says only "millions."

Chapter 08

08What Coachella started

The short versionThe things that left the polo field and kept going: reunions that became world tours (one grossed $584.2M), a second weekend Austin City Limits copied, and a webcast that once made you "like" a gum brand and is now a YouTube exclusive through 2030. Then a look, a suffix, a payment plan, a family of spin-off festivals and a food truck. Coachella rarely invented these things. More often it was the biggest stage they ever got.

In January 2019, Burna Boy found his name in the third line of the Coachella poster, in small type. "I am an AFRICAN GIANT and will not be reduced to whatever that tiny writing means," he wrote on Instagram.244 Later that year he had an album out called African Giant.245 It was nominated for Best World Music Album at the 2020 Grammys.246 The complaint about the small type had become the name of a record.

That's the pattern of this chapter. Coachella rarely invents things outright. It's where they get big, or get named, or get a price tag. Daft Punk's pyramid, the obvious first entry, has its own chapter.

Reunions that became tours

The festival's reputation for reunions starts in 2004. The re-formed Pixies played the main stage before Radiohead and, in Rolling Stone's words, "nearly stole the show."247 It wasn't their first show back. That was a warm-up added on a week's notice at the Fine Line in Minneapolis on April 13, 2004.248 Coachella was the big public test, and they toured on it until late 2005.247

Then came the bigger ones.

Rage Against the Machine, 2007. Coachella confirmed the reunion on January 22, 2007. On April 29 the band closed the festival, their first full show in almost seven years, to "the biggest crowd of the weekend."249 Tom Morello's explanation: "the times were right to see if we can knock the Bush administration out in one fell swoop."250 By Wikipedia's count the tour that followed ran to July 2011, six legs and 50 shows on five continents.251 They were named 2020 headliners too. The pandemic took 2020 and 2021, the 2022 lineup didn't include them, and in January 2024 drummer Brad Wilk wrote that the band "will not be touring or playing live again."252253

Pulp and the Stone Roses. Two dates get mixed up a lot. Pulp re-formed in 2011 for European shows. Their US return came in 2012, built around the two Coachella sets, with Radio City Music Hall on April 11 and the Warfield on April 17.254 The Stone Roses re-formed in 2011 and played their giant UK shows in 2012. Coachella 2013, where they shared the top of the bill with Blur, was their first scheduled US date since.255

Outkast, 2014. Coachella was the first stop of a 20th-anniversary run of more than 40 festivals, with both Fridays to themselves. Big Boi: "It's truly an honor to celebrate 20 years and still be free to do music the way we choose."256

Guns N' Roses, 2016. This is the reunion with the biggest receipt. Axl Rose, Slash and Duff McKagan played a $10 warm-up at the Troubadour in LA on April 1, 2016, then two nights at the new T-Mobile Arena in Las Vegas, then Coachella on April 16 and 23.257 The festival was the big unveiling. The tour, "Not in This Lifetime...," ran until November 2019 and grossed $584.2 million from 5,371,891 tickets. At the time, Billboard ranked it the third-highest-grossing tour in Boxscore history.258

2NE1, 2022. During 88rising's main-stage set, CL brought out Dara, Park Bom and Minzy for "I AM THE BEST." It was the group's first performance together in six years.259 In July 2024 they announced a full reunion and a 15th-anniversary tour.260 The announcement doesn't credit Coachella. The timeline does the talking.

Blink-182, 2023. They were added to the bill late, and their April 14 set was the first reunion show with Tom DeLonge. Mark Hoppus, back from cancer treatment, posted: "Chemo to Coachella. Very much in my feelings today."225 A world tour started that May, and a week after the reunion set they were closing weekend two in Frank Ocean's slot.225

No Doubt, 2024. Weekend one was the band's first show in nearly a decade, and Olivia Rodrigo joined them for "Bathwater." "I didn't think this was really going to happen, did you?" said Gwen Stefani.261 It didn't stop at the polo field. In October 2025 they announced six nights at the Sphere in Las Vegas for May 2026, and the AV Club's report on it pointed straight back to Coachella.262

Two weekends, then everyone

In 2011 the festival sold out so fast that a lot of fans couldn't go. Paul Tollett's choice was to clone the festival instead of growing it. On May 31, 2011, he announced a second, separately ticketed weekend with the same lineup for 2012.32 He gave three reasons. More people wanted in than the site could hold. He didn't want to "ruin the show" by adding about 40,000 people a day. And resale prices of $500-600 struck him as unfair.32

Austin City Limits went to two weekends in 2013. Music Festival Wizard's report described it as "following in the footsteps of mega-festival Coachella."263 Goldenvoice reused the trick itself: Desert Trip, the 2016 classic-rock festival on the same field, added its second weekend after the first sold out.264 The other thing the format produced, the running joke about weekend two, is covered in the moments chapter.

The stream

In 2010, if you wanted to watch Coachella online, you had to "like" the sponsor, Wrigley, on Facebook, and you got one act at a time.265 A year later YouTube streamed three stages on separate channels, about 60 bands over three days, and nearly 4 million people watched. Ad Age's Andrew Hampp estimated the sponsorship cost in the mid-to-high six figures.265 Nine Inch Nails' Rob Sheridan summed up the logic: "An experience is the best kind of scarcity."265

Within three months YouTube had signed up to stream Lollapalooza and ACL as well.266 Coachella kept setting the records. Beyoncé's 2018 set peaked at about 458,000 people watching at once, by TMZ's figures as The FADER reported them.212 In September 2026 YouTube extended its exclusive global deal through 2030, both weekends. It said the 2026 stream had set its record for concurrent viewers, though it didn't give the number.267

Holograms after Tupac

The 2012 Tupac was a Pepper's ghost on Mylar, not a hologram, and it's told in the moments chapter. Dr. Dre said it was "strictly for Coachella."205 The idea didn't stay there.

In 2014, Hologram USA and Musion Das went to court to block a Michael Jackson projection at the Billboard Music Awards. They claimed patents on "the technology that brought Tupac to Coachella 2012." They lost.268 In 2018, BASE Hologram put a projected Roy Orbison on a 28-date tour, with Tupac at Coachella explicitly cited as the precedent.269 People sometimes connect all this to ABBA Voyage. No source we found makes that link, and Voyage uses different technology, so it stays out.

The Sahara and the boom

The lore · The tent that started the boom

The legend goes that Coachella's dance tent made American EDM. There's a lot of truth in it. The Sahara is almost as old as the festival, and its first bill was A-Trak, Juan Atkins, Kevin Saunderson and Richie Hawtin, out at the far edge of the grounds.82 In 2010 deadmau5 played it on a Friday night opposite Jay-Z, with "bombastic lights and razor-edged electro-house."238 The LA Times credits the tent with helping pop-EDM break wide open: Avicii, Skrillex, Calvin Harris.82 In 2012 Swedish House Mafia became the first DJ group to headline the main stage.128 By 2018 the tent had been moved to the middle of the field and made 25% bigger.82

The careful version comes from Festival Insider. Electronic music at Coachella "roared back" in 2011, it says, in a timeline it describes as "coinciding with" the national EDM boom.128 Coinciding is a modest word for a 250-foot-wide stage.82 Beatport, YouTube and social media did a lot of the pushing.128 The Sahara was where the boom came to be seen, which is most of what a festival can do for anything.

Firsts on the main stage

Bad Bunny. At his 2019 debut, by Wikipedia's account of Billboard's report, he asked the crowd, "I want to know where the Latinos are!"270 In 2023 he came back as the first Latin artist to headline.271 Three years later Karol G became the first Latina headliner.272 She closed the 2026 festival, with Becky G, Mariah Angeliq and Wisin as guests on weekend one.273

BLACKPINK. In 2019 they were the first K-pop girl group to play the festival. In 2023 they were the first K-pop group to be billed as headliners.271 (Some outlets said "first Asian headliner." The careful sources say K-pop.) The 2022 lineup had already shown where things were going. 88rising's "Head in the Clouds Forever" was, per Billboard, "the first label-curated set ever to hit the festival's main stage," and it brought out Hikaru Utada, who had "never played a festival, even in Asia."274 It went the other way round from what people assume. 88rising's own festival, Head in the Clouds, came first, in 2018. Wikipedia notes that outlets nicknamed it the "Asian Coachella."275

Afrobeats. In 2019, Burna Boy and Mr Eazi were the only African acts on the bill.276 Quartz noted that Wizkid had been booked the year before and didn't appear, citing visa trouble, and that Black Coffee's 2016 debut got him rebooked on a bigger stage in 2018.276 Seven years after the small type, Wizkid and Tems came out during Justin Bieber's headline set.240

The look

Coachella did not invent boho. CTV traces the look to Sienna Miller at Glastonbury in 2004 and to the runways of 2003-05: Chloé, Cavalli, Bottega Veneta.277 What Coachella did was give it a name and a backdrop. By the mid-2000s, Dazed writes, celebrity attendees including Paris Hilton had made boho "Coachella fashion." Then Instagram arrived:

"Coachella fashion was no longer just about the clothes. It was about content."278

NYLON's year-by-year timeline runs from forehead headbands (2009) through "a sea of flower crowns," all-white crochet credited to "the Vanessa Hudgens effect" (2018) and florals "boosted by the Revolve Festival" (2019), to "cowboy cosplay" (2024).279 In 2015 H&M made it official with what it called the "first ever co-branded collection" with the festival, in about 350 North American stores.280

The look also had a problem: faux Native American war bonnets. On April 9, 2014, Victoria's Secret model Alessandra Ambrosio posted a selfie in a feathered headdress, captioned as getting inspired for Coachella.281 The bans that followed came from other festivals. Coachella itself never adopted one, per CTV.277

  • Bass Coast (British Columbia) posted the first widely reported ban, in July 2014. "Our core team felt it was time to take a stand."282
  • Glastonbury banned the sale of headdresses in October 2014, after a Change.org petition with 65 signatures.283
  • Osheaga, Heavy Montreal and ÎleSoniq followed in July 2015.284
  • Electric Forest banned them from 2016.285
  • Outside Lands added them to its prohibited list in 2019, "out of respect for Native American heritage and culture."286

By 2019, CTV wrote, "the most offensive examples had largely disappeared from Coachella."277

The influencers, and the brand houses

Heineken was the first big sponsor with branded experiences on site, in 2006, according to Hypebeast. H&M, Amex and Absolut followed, and around 2014-15 the influencers arrived with stylists and brand-paid houses. Hypebeast's verdict: "Coachella has become the influencer's Met Gala."287 By 2012, the first two-weekend year, BizBash listed a crowded weekend-one party circuit: Lacoste pool parties, the Guess Hotel in La Quinta, Neon Carnival in a hangar in Thermal. The brands stuck to weekend one for the media and the feeling of being there first.288

The biggest player was Revolve. It first flew influencers to the festival in 2013.289 Its own Revolve Festival started in 2015 at the Avalon in Palm Springs, according to WWD; Business of Fashion dates it to 2016.290289 By 2022 it was a two-day invite-only festival with headliners like Cardi B, SZA and Travis Scott, peaking at about 5,000 guests. BoF's headline put it plainly: "Revolve invented influencer marketing as we know it."289 The season was worth real money. Revolve "typically earns about 30% of its yearly revenue in the weeks around Coachella," Retail Brew reported in 2020, citing the New York Times.291

Then the pullback. In 2019 Sephora, H&M, Urban Decay, Too Faced, Kiehl's and Maybelline scaled back or dropped their VIP experiences. One consultant told Glossy: "A $1,000 VIP wristband doesn't equate to a social campaign that is usually more than $100,000."292 In 2022 Revolve's own invited guests were left waiting for shuttles in the heat, and the internet called it Fyre Fest. LA Magazine's Joseph Kapsch described being "stranded in the dirt with no water, under the hot sun for hours." Revolve apologized.293 By 2024 the festival was down to one day and about 1,200 guests.289 Big branded venues shrank. Influencer budgets, Dazed reported in 2026, did not, and the top ones reportedly run from "high six figures to over a million dollars."278

"-chella"

The linguist Arnold Zwicky files "-chella" as a libfix, a piece of a word that broke off and now means "festival" on its own. His examples include #Bookchella for the LA Times Festival of Books.294 Fans coined Beychella in 2018, and Dictionary.com logged Arichella for Ariana Grande a year later.213

Goldenvoice's lawyers have kept busy with it. The festival sued Hoodchella, an underground LA one-dayer, in 2016, seeking an injunction and more than $100K.295 Brokechella, a DIY LA festival started in 2011 as a "bargain alternative," became BROKE LA in January 2016. "We got too big and they wouldn't let us be Brokechella anymore," its organizer said, without naming who "they" were.296 In Wales, a Cowbridge festival called Cowchella became Moo-La-La after a complaint from Coachella, according to a UK promotions trade blog. Its founder, Ian "H" Watkins of Steps, called himself "a threat to their ginormous, juggernaut of a machine."297 In 2025 a dog-friendly festival got a cease-and-desist, which World Trademark Review called "just the latest" over the suffix.298 Some got away with it. In October 2023, a pub in Collie, Western Australia, held Col-Chella, a one-day festival with a stage on the back of a truck.299

Paying for it

In 2009, in the middle of a recession, Coachella offered a layaway plan. It came back for 2010 with a choice of 50% or 10% down and automatic installments through February 15.300 By 2025 about 60% of the crowd was using the payment plan, according to a Billboard report as relayed by Entrepreneur.301

The other direction was up. By April 2010 you could reserve a "Coachella Safari Tent": a queen bed, air conditioning, power outlets, VIP wristbands, a shuttle, backstage access, a 2 a.m. curfew and one case of beer per person.302 Palm Springs Life later counted 100 VIP safari tents at about $6,500 a weekend for two.303 By 2026 the ladder ran from $160 car camping through GA at $649 for weekend one to Lake Eldorado lodges at $2,300 and tents at $3,000.46 Nobody we read credits Coachella with inventing glamping. It did sell it early, and it turned it into a product line.

A family of festivals

Goldenvoice used Coachella's field, crew and name to start a run of other festivals, each with a narrower audience.

  • Stagecoach, the country festival on the same grounds the weekend after, started in 2007, per Wikipedia.304 In 2024 it sold 89,003 tickets, more than either Coachella weekend that year.55
  • Desert Trip, October 2016: Dylan, the Stones, McCartney, Neil Young, The Who and Roger Waters, nicknamed "Oldchella." Tollett told the New York Times it grossed about $160M, "the highest-grossing show by far."305264
  • Power Trip, October 2023: the same format for metal, with Iron Maiden, Guns N' Roses, Judas Priest, AC/DC, Tool and Metallica.116
  • Just Like Heaven (2019), a lineup of Coachella alumni, and Cruel World, the goth and new wave one, which Wikipedia dates to 2022 after a cancelled 2020 debut.306307

Goldenvoice's own festivals page now lists more than a dozen, from Portola in San Francisco to Head in the Clouds and Splash House.308 It didn't invent the nostalgia festival. InsideHook points to All Tomorrow's Parties as an earlier example. It did help turn the idea into an industry.309

The food

Roy Choi's Kogi truck came to Coachella around 2009, and UPROXX credits it with helping push festival food toward chef-driven vendors.310 The festival's real food push came later, and it started rough. In 2014 Coachella brought in LA chefs including Josef Centeno and Kris Yenbamroong of Night + Market. Yenbamroong said he threw away about 75% of his food: "people just weren't into it."311 A year later the festival had $225 Outstanding in the Field dinners in the Rose Garden.311 By 2016 those cost $225 on their own, $600 with GA or $1,124 with VIP.312

Other festivals took up the idea. KAABOO's CEO, Jason Felts: "Chefs are the new rock stars."310 Coachella wasn't first, though. LA Weekly noted in 2015 that Outside Lands and Jazz Fest already ranked food just behind music, and that Coachella ranked it lower.311 What Coachella did was give the chefs top billing and put the dinners in a garden.

Still open

  • Darker Waves, the new wave festival in Huntington Beach, looks like a rival's copy of Cruel World: Pollstar's headline credits C3 Presents, Live Nation's festival arm. We haven't been able to read the piece.
  • Where the 60% payment-plan figure first came from, and whether it covers GA only.
  • Who started the Coachella flower crown. Nobody on record says.

Chapter 09

09Getting there

The short versionAbout 40,000 people a day ride a shuttle into Indio, and the company that runs it isn't Goldenvoice - it's a travel outfit owned, three layers up, by private equity. The drivers come from all over, the train ran exactly once, and the rich fly into a WWII airfield in Thermal. Everyone else sits on Jefferson Street, then leaves on Monday at 9 a.m.

The bus you ride to Coachella belongs to a company named after a man named Dan. Not literally - the drivers are subcontracted - but follow the shuttle pass up the chain and you reach CID Entertainment, which stands for "Consider It Dan," started by the tour manager of a Philadelphia jam band.313 Above that sits a sports hospitality firm, and above that, four private-equity investors.314 None of them is Goldenvoice.

For two weekends every April, Indio has to take in a crowd the size of a small city every afternoon, and send it back out after midnight. The 2019 traffic plan assumed 125,000 people a day.315 This chapter is about how they get there: the buses, the roads, the train that ran once, and the private jets.

The shuttle and the man named Dan

Valley Music Travel (VMT) runs the official shuttles. It sells the passes, staffs the stops and answers the phone.316 It calls itself the "Official Enhanced Experience Partner of Coachella," and sells black cars and in-venue carts on the side.317 Its own About page describes it as "a subsidiary of CID Entertainment."318

CID's founder, Dan Berkowitz, became tour manager for the Disco Biscuits in 2004. He noticed most bands didn't offer travel and VIP packages to fans, so he started a company that would. He worked out of an office in the band's studio, and in 2008 sold a small stake for $100,000 to grow it.313 Pollstar puts the founding at 2007.319 By 2015 CID had about 60 full-time staff in Philadelphia, and up to 300 people working for it over a big festival weekend. At Coachella, it would "build you a 14-by-14-foot safari tent with full amenities on site."313

In October 2018, On Location Experiences bought CID, according to Berkowitz.319 On Location now calls CID "a division of On Location," and lists its own owners as RedBird Capital Partners, Bruin Sports Capital, 32 Equity and the Carlyle Group.314 So the chain runs: your shuttle pass, VMT, CID, On Location, private equity. No source shows Goldenvoice or AEG holding a stake in any link of it, and no source rules it out either.

VMT has done the job since at least 2010. That spring Pollstar wrote that "Coachella partnered with Valley Music Travel" for hotel shuttles. 2010 was also the first year everyone got in-and-out privileges, which is what made a hotel shuttle worth having.320 Goldenvoice used VMT again for Desert Trip in 2016: a $25 pass, over 20 stops, and a shuttle coordinator at every pickup.321

The pass

The product is the "Any Line" pass: one pass, good from any stop, all weekend. Stops sit about 5 to 25 miles from the polo grounds, and not every hotel is one of them. Peak inbound is 2 to 5 p.m. Peak outbound is midnight to 2 a.m., when VMT says it runs "its full fleet."316

The price has gone up about two and a half times in eleven years.

By the numbers · The shuttle pass, per weekend

  • 2015: $60, or $435 bundled with GA322
  • 2017: $75, and sold out323
  • 2019: $80324
  • 2026: $150 round trip; GA plus shuttle $779 for weekend 1, $679 for weekend 246
  • Riders in 2026: about 40,000, by the City of Indio's estimate325

The stops are mostly resort hotels down Highway 111. A typical list - this one is VMT's Stagecoach list, which follows the same pattern - runs from the Hampton Inn in Indio through the Hyatt and Renaissance in Indian Wells, the JW Marriott Desert Springs, La Quinta Resort, the Westin in Rancho Mirage and an Albertsons on 111. Three free park-and-rides fill the gaps: the Indian Wells Tennis Garden, Agua Caliente Casino and the Palm Springs Convention Center. Children 10 and under ride free with an adult.326 In 2015 the list still reached the Palm Springs hotels and a campground at Lake Cahuilla.322

The Tennis Garden is the real hub. It's VMT's will-call, where people pick up wristbands, and a free lot.316 On Friday, Washington Street outside it becomes a choke point, as people collect wristbands and board buses.327

For people flying in, there's an LAX shuttle from Terminal 1 baggage claim to the hotel stops, with a separate camping-only version. VMT puts the drive at about three hours without traffic, and says plainly it won't cover a missed flight.316 In 2017 it cost $70 each way.323 Hotel packages bundle the room, the festival pass and the shuttle; a 2022 list ran from a Residence Inn 4.4 miles away to a Hilton in Palm Springs, 26.4 miles out.328

The buses drop you at the shuttle lot, about a 10-minute walk from the entrance, with restrooms and lines signed by stop.316 The festival's own map puts the Shuttle Lobby on the Yellow Path.329

The drivers come from everywhere

Here is what isn't public: how many coaches there are, how many drivers, which charter companies supply them, and where they sleep. Nothing published gives a fleet count. The closest thing to one came by accident.

On Sunday night, April 24, 2022, the shuttle drivers ate a catered dinner of pasta and pre-packaged salad at the Riverside County fairgrounds in Indio. Calls started shortly after midnight. Responders treated drivers at the fairgrounds, at "the shuttle bus lot," at hotels and at pickup points across the valley. Forty-six went to hospital: 23 to Eisenhower, 12 to Desert Regional and 11 to JFK Memorial. County health said "more than 100 drivers report being sick."330 The drivers were subcontracted. The caterer was never named, and officials said it had nothing to do with the festival.330

So the driver pool is well over 100 people. That's an inference from a food-poisoning count, which tells you how little is published about the fleet. What the outbreak does show is the shape of the thing: a festival-travel company running a subcontracted driver pool for two weekends, then a third for Stagecoach, which VMT also runs on the same hotel-and-park-and-ride model.326

From the field · Richard

I was once boarding a coach in Boston, bound for New Hampshire, wearing a Coachella shirt. The driver turned out to have driven for Valley Music Travel - at Coachella and at Stagecoach.

The train that ran once

Indio used to be a railroad town. Southern Pacific reached it in 1876. Amtrak's Sunset Limited stopped there until October 1998, when, by Wikipedia's account of the station, it closed for "low ridership."331

Ten years later, Coachella got one train. On Thursday, April 24, 2008, a chartered Amtrak train left Los Angeles Union Station for a temporary platform in Indio, arriving at 5 p.m. It had seven cars, including a business-class coach, a café car and a Superliner lounge, and was led by F59PH locomotive No. 458.332 About 300 festivalgoers were aboard.332 Riders who had bought three-day passes rode free, and Goldenvoice handed everyone VIP passes for all three days.333

It was a party. DJs set up laptops on the stainless steel counters in the back cars, and Junkie XL played breakbeats to a packed car. Rosanna Arquette hosted, followed by a camera crew Goldenvoice had hired. The writer on board for LA Weekly said "the three hours flew by."333 At the platform, Indio's mayor said a transportation center would be built there, and an Amtrak vice president agreed that desert passenger service "could and should happen."332

It didn't. In 2016 the county transportation commission floated festival trains for 2017 and 2018, under a roughly $1.2M "convenience plan"; the headlines called it "Choo-Choo-Chella."334 The trains never ran. In 2019 the state awarded the Riverside County Transportation Commission $5.9M in rail money, and the commission added $2.7M, for $8.6M in all. That would have paid for a 900-foot temporary platform at the Indio Transit Center, with festival trains from Union Station "as early as 2020."335 Then 2020 happened. Wikipedia, citing the Desert Sun, says the plan was cancelled that year, amid the pandemic, after arrangements with Union Pacific fell through.331 The platform was never built.

The long game is Coachella Valley Rail, a proposed daily service on about 144 miles from Los Angeles with nine stops. In January 2026 the commission awarded an $80M contract for its environmental review. The project will likely cost more than $1.5B, and service could start "by 2040."336 Nobody has promised DJs.

The fast way in

If you have the money, you skip Interstate 10 altogether and land in Thermal.

Jacqueline Cochran Regional Airport, in Thermal, was built in 1942 as a WWII Army airfield. It has an 8,500-foot runway, and Wikipedia's entry on it says its busy season is the horse show circuit, December to March - not April.337 In April, the pop-ups arrive.

  • 2016: Uber and Blade flew helicopters from Van Nuys for $4,170 one way, or $695 a seat with six aboard, with an Uber for the last leg and a tequila bar before takeoff. Uber's pitch: "There's no traffic 1,000 feet in the air."338
  • 2017: XOJET flights to three desert airports were up 40%, according to a Quartz report cited by an industry site.339
  • 2022: Blade said it was flying roughly 200 passengers into Thermal from Santa Monica, turning "a five-hour car ride into only 40 minutes in the air."340
  • 2026: JSX ran Santa Monica to Thermal on April 9, back on April 13, nonstop in 50 minutes, for $606 each way.341

No one publishes how many private jets land on festival weekends.

The commercial airport, Palm Springs International, feels it in fares rather than records. In one festival year (the San Francisco Chronicle's piece is undated, likely 2019), round trips from San Francisco ran about $600 to $800 for weekend 1 and $500 to $600 for weekend 2, against about $320 in early April.342 The airport's busiest month ever, with nearly 500,000 passengers, was March 2025 - the month before the festival.343

Down on the ground, the top of the price ladder is a staging pass for your hired car. For 2026, the festival charged $3,500 for a vehicle with 10 seats or fewer, and $5,000 for anything bigger, up to a 35-foot bus - "for drivers to service pre-booked clients only."329 The sky above the grounds is closed: a federal flight restriction banned drones from April 9 to 13, 2026.325

The roads

Most people drive. Indio prepares by closing its own streets.

The long closure is Avenue 50 between Monroe and Madison, shut for about six weeks, through both Coachella weekends and Stagecoach. In 2024 it ran from March 25 to May 6. "The closure of Avenue 50 is essential to ensure the safety of workers, residents, and commuters alike," said Jim Curtis, the city's community services manager.344 In 2026 it closed March 23 and reopened after May 4.345

Then, Friday to Monday of each festival weekend, four more go:346

  • Avenue 49, between Hjorth and Monroe
  • Avenue 50, between Madison and Jackson
  • Hjorth Street, between Avenues 49 and 50
  • Madison Street, between Avenues 49 and 52

It's the same list Indio published in 2019.324 Inbound traffic peaks from about 3 to 8 p.m., Friday to Sunday, on Jefferson, Washington and Monroe. Outbound runs from about midnight to 3 a.m.327 Caltrans watches the climb in from the coast: I-10 from Beaumont and SR-60 from Moreno Valley, eastbound peaking Friday and westbound Sunday.315

The police side is a numbers game. Indio PD's traffic division runs festival enforcement. In a crackdown briefing in April 2025, it cited 112 arrests and 83 citations for misused disabled placards, and a 40% spike in arrests on the year before.347 Weekend 2 of 2026 ended with 106 arrests and 87 placard citations, and when festival lots filled, police sent the overflow to the Tennis Garden.348

The last mile

Wherever you start, you finish on foot. The festival sorts arrivals by lot. Rideshare goes to Lot 1D at Avenue 49 and Monroe, open 11 a.m. to 3 a.m., with the longest waits from midnight to 2:30. Friends and taxis drop at Lot 13, at Avenue 52 and Madison. Walkers and cyclists get four color-coded entries - Red, Green, Yellow and Blue - and no pedestrians are allowed on Monroe between Avenues 49 and 52. A bike locked anywhere but a rack "will be removed and forfeited." Day parking isn't guaranteed, and "Cars left after 2am will be towed."329

The cheerful exception is Carpoolchella, a program run with the nonprofit Global Inheritance. Cars with four or more people, decorated and spotted on the way in, can win backstage passes, VIP upgrades or "VIP tickets for life."329349 It's an incentive to fill the car, wrapped as a contest. In 2026, cars in a grocery store lot in Indio were already decorated for it at 1 a.m. on Thursday.350 Global Inheritance also organized the 2008 train.349

On the way in, you'll pass the billboards. They're the subject of their own chapter: the I-10 billboard archive.

The line

The worst wait in the festival's recent history wasn't for a stage. It was for the campground.

On Thursday, April 10, 2025, campers sat in their cars for 8, 10, 12 hours. One told KESQ she'd arrived at 3 a.m. and it was almost 11. Another said, "Seven hours into this." The first: "I've seen people pass out through heat exhaustion, it's been chaos."351 There were no restrooms, and some people relieved themselves on the roadside, in a heat wave.352

Five days later, Goldenvoice's head of public safety, George Cunningham, went to the La Quinta City Council. His explanation: the usual midday lull never came. "Everybody wanted to come early." Three things had stacked up. The festival's first-ever Thursday night campground concert pulled people in early. Unlike some past years, nobody was let in before the official 9 a.m. opening, so the queue backed onto public roads. And not every checkpoint was fully staffed.353 Then he apologized: "Again, [we] apologize for what took place to your residents, the impact that it had to the community."352

The fix was more booths. For weekend 2, Goldenvoice added 12 toll plazas near Madison and Avenue 50, for 44 in all.353 The camping chapter picks up the line from inside the car.

Monday

The festival ends on Sunday night. The exodus is Monday morning.

More than 40,000 campers leave after each weekend.325 It's been about that many for years: Indio expected "nearly 40,000" in 2019.324 Camper gates open around 8:30 to 9 a.m., and Washington, Jefferson and Monroe fill at once. Things usually ease by about 11:30. The city's advice for anyone heading west is to be on I-10 "before 9 a.m." After that, expect the freeway to be gridlocked for most of the day.327

Then the roads reopen, the buses go back to wherever they came from, and Indio does it again the next Thursday.

Still open

  • How many coaches and drivers VMT runs, and which charter companies supply them.
  • When CID took over VMT. VMT says it's a subsidiary; no announcement has turned up.
  • Where the buses go between weekend 2 and Stagecoach.
  • Any count of private jet landings at Thermal on festival weekends.

Chapter 10

10Camping and the rituals

The short versionCamping began in 2003 and cars joined in 2010; now about 35,000 people sleep on the grounds. The festival starts for them on Thursday - in a Walmart lot, a Ralphs lot, a 12-hour line, and since 2025 at Goldenvoice's own campers-only party, Day 0. Along the way: a gospel service on a hill built in a week, horses, hot dogs, an iron fence and a wristband scam.

At 1 a.m. on the Thursday of Coachella 2026, one of the best parties in Indio was in the parking lot of a Ralphs. There were porta-potties, cars painted for the Carpoolchella contest, people doing their last grocery run, and a DJ set by NDA. Nobody official organized it. Word spread on the Coachella subreddit, and it has happened "for the past few years."350 The campground opened eight hours later.

For the roughly 35,000 people who camp,140 Coachella doesn't start with a headliner. It starts with a line, a lot and a Thursday. This chapter is the festival before the festival.

Tents first, cars later

Camping came early, but not at the start. Wikipedia, citing coverage from the time, says Indio approved overnight camping for 2003, for up to four people on each of 2,252 spots.18 An OC Weekly writer's history of the campgrounds backs the timing: 2004 "was the second year camping was permitted, changing the entire dynamic of the festival."25 Her account of that era is a party where nobody slept.25

Cars arrived with the 2010 festival. Before that, you parked and hauled your gear to the tent area. From 2010 you could pitch beside your car on a 10-by-30-foot space, or just sleep in it.354 The same year brought a farmers market by the general store, a roller rink called the Down & Derby, and $5,000 "Safari" tents with air conditioning, showers and golf-cart shuttles.30

The 2026 menu runs from plain car camping up through Preferred, Front Row and Powered sites (one outlet), group sites new that year, pre-pitched tents, temperature-controlled La Campana tents with private showers, and Lake Eldorado, with its own check-in, concierge and restrooms.355 For 2027, a car site is $160 ($40 a night), and Lake Eldorado runs $2,300 to $3,000.356

The rules shape the weekend. Camping opens at 9 a.m. Thursday and closes at 9 a.m. Monday. Once your car is parked, it doesn't move. From 9 p.m. to 2 a.m., Friday to Sunday, no vehicle leaves - the "lockdown." Groups in the better tiers must "Arrive Together to Camp Together," driving through the toll booth and the search as one, and saving spots isn't allowed. Quiet hours start at 1:30 a.m.355 The one loophole is new: the 2026 Group Car Camping tier, 10 to 20 spots in Lot 5A, lets members arrive separately.355

Then it's a small town for four days. The general store in Lot 8 sells Panera bread delivered every morning, plus donuts, sandwiches, fruit and ice. The farmers market, run by Flutterby Ranch, sells produce and live butterflies. Food trucks open at 2 p.m. Thursday, a free shuttle runs from Lot 7 to the supermarket, and ice comes to you on wheels.355 There's a lounge with Wi-Fi.357 Nobody is roughing it, exactly, but nobody is moving their car either.

The expensive end

At the top, camping stops being camping. The 2010 Safari tents cost $5,000.30 A Palm Springs Life feature, undated, put a Safari weekend at about $6,500, with two wristbands, golf-cart shuttles, air-conditioned tents, a marquee with breakfast and a DJ, a concierge, and $1,500 for each extra guest.303 For 2026, one guide put a Safari cabana at "upwards of $10,000."92

Some of the tents are built by the same company that runs the shuttles. CID Entertainment, which Valley Music Travel's own site names as its parent,318 told the Philadelphia Inquirer in 2015 that at Coachella it would "build you a 14-by-14-foot safari tent with full amenities on site."313 Your bus and your bed can share an owner.

The night before

The reason for the waiting is in the rules. Cars and campers are searched at check-in, and sites are handed out in order of arrival within each tier.355 Show up first, camp closest. Check-in isn't always quick: a long-time camper told OC Weekly in 2018 that "there were only about 4 workers directing everyone where to park."25 So people arrive early and wait somewhere. Where they wait has become its own tradition.

The first famous version was an accident. On weekend 2 of 2018, a high-wind warning, with gusts forecast to 60 mph, pushed camping from 9 a.m. Thursday to about 3 a.m. Friday - roughly 18 hours. Thousands of people waited in a Walmart lot in Indio with folding chairs and portable barbecues, and tagged it #Walmartchella. Indio officers reportedly joined in a beanbag game.358

In La Quinta, next door, it went differently. Riverside County deputies told campers to leave the La Quinta Walmart early Thursday, citing a La Quinta ordinance against sleeping in vehicles. Indio said it would be lenient; a police sergeant said campers "may need to seek refuge in parking lots."359 By the end of the day the cities had published a list of 12 temporary waiting sites, including a fashion mall, a Home Depot, two casinos and the campsites at Lake Cahuilla. La Quinta City Hall offered its south lot - 500-plus spots and restrooms, but no tents and no open flames. In the city of Coachella, the Village 29 campground took up to 1,000.360

By 2026 the waiting had a home. The Ralphs on Jefferson Street is the unofficial Day 0: the 1 a.m. party, the porta-potties, the decorated cars.350 One regular, KC, remembered their first year there, when police "couldn't stay here for longer than 30 minutes without them knocking on your windows" (reported by attendees).350 Officially, camping opens at 9 a.m. In practice, the Independent reported, security "usually lets people in around 3 a.m."350

The line

That "usually" matters. In 2025 it didn't happen.

On Thursday, April 10, 2025, campers waited in their cars for up to 12 hours or more, in a heat wave, with no restrooms.352 One said she'd arrived at 3 a.m. and was still waiting near 11: "I've seen people pass out through heat exhaustion, it's been chaos."351 Goldenvoice's head of public safety later told La Quinta's council that the usual midday lull never came, that not every checkpoint was fully staffed, and that, unlike some past years, nobody was let in before 9 a.m. The other cause was new: the first-ever Thursday night concert in the campground, which made everyone want to arrive early.353 He apologized, and weekend 2 got 12 more toll plazas.353 The full story is in Getting there.

Day 0

That Thursday concert is the newest ritual, and and one Goldenvoice built on purpose.

"Day 0" is what campers call the Thursday they arrive; the Independent's Ralphs report was filed from "Day 0 of Coachella 2026."350 In 2025, Goldenvoice gave it a stage. Chris Lake played the first-ever Thursday night show inside the campgrounds on weekend 1, and on weekend 2 he was swapped for Disco Dom, Dombresky's disco project, at 8:30 p.m.361 It was the party that helped cause the 12-hour line.353 Goldenvoice kept it anyway. For 2026, Disco Lines was announced as the weekend 1 Day Zero performer, an exclusive pre-festival set for campers, second year running.362 Weekend 2 got KETTAMA, Prospa and Josh Baker playing back to back to back.363

Around it, the Thursday campground now has a full program: DJs at Desert Sky from 8 p.m., a silent disco until 3 a.m., and stargazing on Coyote Hill through NASA-supplied telescopes.357 The name isn't Coachella's alone.364

The rest of the weekend has its own campground calendar. In 2026 that meant an activities tent, a recovery zone with massage and ice baths, a silent disco until 4 a.m. Friday to Sunday, a water balloon fight at noon on Sunday, and a Euphoria season 3 screening in the campgrounds at 11:59 p.m. on Sunday, April 12.357

Sunday Service on the Mountain

The campground's most famous morning was Easter Sunday, April 21, 2019, at 9 a.m.

Kanye West brought his Sunday Service to a spot in the campground dubbed "the Mountain." Paul Tollett told Billboard where the idea came from: "Why don't we try this in camping, since it's in the morning and all the other campers are already there?"218 The hill was new. Tollett said the dirt went in on the Tuesday morning between weekends, and the sod on Thursday night and Friday. His older brother, Perry, designed the hidden loudspeakers.365

Variety described a circular mound beside a cordoned-off stretch of the campground. A robed choir sang on the slopes, with dancers on the surrounding hills, a harp, horns and at least five percussionists. West debuted "Water," and the set ran through "Jesus Walks," "Ultralight Beam," Kirk Franklin's "Brighter Day" and Stevie Wonder's "As." The merch read "Sunday Service at the Mountain."220 The merch tent was called "Church Clothes."366

The lore · The 50,000

The legend goes that "over 50,000" people climbed out of their tents for a 9 a.m. church service. That number comes from TMZ, citing unnamed Coachella production sources, the day after.367 No other count has turned up. On a hill built in four days, on Easter, with the campers already there, it doesn't need one.

It hasn't been repeated. No other gospel or church service in the campgrounds has turned up, and the 2026 program lists none.357

Horses, guards and drones

The campground has its own police presence, some of it on horseback. In 2018, OC Weekly reported mounted officers patrolling the campgrounds. The agencies on site that year ran from Indio PD, in the lead, through Palm Springs, Cathedral City, Desert Hot Springs, Banning and Beaumont police, the Riverside County Sheriff, the CHP and the FBI. There were armed observation posts above the VIP area and the Outdoor Theatre, and drones over traffic.368 Which agency the horses belonged to, the piece doesn't say.

Indio PD still flew drones for crowd monitoring in 2026.369 Inside the gates, in 2025, close to 1,000 Allied Universal guards worked around the clock, and Indio PD announced "zero tolerance."370 Weekend 1 of 2026 ended with 97 arrests, mostly for drug possession and fake IDs.371

The lore · The 2007 riot

The legend goes that the campground rioted after Rage Against the Machine closed the 2007 festival. The source is one OC Weekly writer's 2018 history, built from attendee posts on the Coachella forum: helicopters overhead, SWAT nearby, a Taser.25 The next year, by the same account, brought heavy multi-agency patrols and a 2 a.m. curfew, which people mostly partied through.25 Research for this chapter found no police report on the night, and no second account of it. By 2014, the same writer says, the campground had gone much quieter at night.25

The hot dogs

Outside the fence, the most local ritual is a bacon-wrapped hot dog at 2 a.m.

Local vendors used to sell along every path, at any hour: hot dogs, stacked 24-packs of water, shaded cooler stands. A Phoenix New Times piece from around 2016 tracked their decline. Once Madison and Monroe between Avenues 48 and 52 were closed to foot traffic, citing safety, the hot dog carts were pushed to the end of the night and to the northeast lots at the end of the Yellow Path.372 The water kids were mostly gone.372

In 2025, they were still there, and still fast.

The lore · The storming

The legend goes that on April 13, 2025, as weekend 1 let out, the hot dog vendors "stormed" Coachella. TikTok videos show vendors racing toward the exit to set up for the crowd. One caption said they were "breaking down the fence," and that security threatened pepper spray. Another said they knocked down an entire wall. Distractify, which collected the videos, notes "the destruction claims aren't confirmed" (reported by attendees).373 What the videos do show is a sprint, and a market.

Fences and wristbands

The festival learned to lock itself in the hard way.

In 2010, Goldenvoice dropped single-day tickets for the first time and sold only three-day passes, at $269, with wristbands meant to stop scalpers. All 75,000 sold.30 Daily attendance ran about 75,000, up about 15,000 a day from 2009,374 and reports from that year describe long holdups at the front gates and worries about gate-crashing.374

The response came in 2011: a wrought-iron fence around the Empire Polo grounds to stop gate crashers, an extra traffic lane partly paid for by Goldenvoice, and the end of exchanging a ticket for a wristband at the gate.31 The wristband became the ticket.

Then it got a chip. By 2012, RFID wristbands from Intellitix handled access at both weekends, which a trade site said "eliminates counterfeit tickets," and let fans check in on social media automatically.375 The vendor itself said in 2014 that it had supplied Coachella's RFID for four years, which puts the start around 2010 or 2011.376 2014 was the first year everyone had to register their wristband online.376

Binding a wristband to its buyer opened a new hustle. As early as 2014, the CV Independent described the scam: a seller reports a delivered pass as "never received," gets a replacement, and sells the original, which then gets switched off.377 In 2026 it got a name. "Scamchella," NBC Palm Springs called it: sellers on Facebook Marketplace and OfferUp sold real wristbands, then reported them lost or stolen. One buyer, Ryan Jimenez, paid more than $2,000 for three. They worked on day one and were dead on day two. Hundreds lined up at will-call, unsure their passes would work.378 He did get his money back.378

The heat

The weather is why Coachella is in April at all. The first festival, in early October 1999, ran in "blazing triple-digit temperatures."4 When it came back in 2001, it was one day, "scheduled for the milder weather of April."4 Britannica says the move was partly about avoiding the heat.379

April is milder, not mild. JFK Memorial Hospital in Indio, the closest one, adds staff every year and sees heat illness alongside drug and alcohol cases. "Many of these emergency visits are preventable," its medical director said in 2026.380 The 2025 camping line happened in a heat wave.352 And the desert has another trick: wind. It's what kept the campground shut for 18 hours in 2018.358

The neighbors

For the people who live next to the polo grounds, the rituals are economic. In 2016, OC Weekly talked to neighbors who rent out rooms, sheds and yard space at premium prices, and who say their houses shake from the bass. One said Goldenvoice wanted her block. Residents could get discounted tickets, but "nothing more."381

Hotels across the valley run more than 90% full in April, which Expedia data says is the highest-rate month of the year.382 Some luxury homes reportedly rent for "more than $100,000" for one weekend - an anecdote from homeowners and agents, not data.383

The camper, meanwhile, leaves on Monday with more than 40,000 others,325 and by the next Thursday somebody else is waiting in a parking lot.

Still open

  • Which agency, or which private firm, rides the horses in the campgrounds.
  • When the Ralphs party started, and who first posted it.
  • The first year of RFID wristbands at Coachella: 2010, 2011 or 2012.

Chapter 11

11The lineup and the poster

The short versionOne man reads thousands of pitches a year, picks about 150 acts, and sets every name on the poster in one typeface at one of several sizes - a size agents fight over. The poster used to land in January. Now it lands in the fall, and the 2027 passes went on sale with no names on them at all. Behind it sits a radius clause that keeps those names off other stages from December to May, and a small Portland festival that sued over it.

Until Jan. 1, 2019, Goldenvoice was making a Coachella poster with Kanye West at the top of it. The lineup came out on Jan. 2 without him.24384 Paul Tollett told the Los Angeles Times that West's production ideas were "great," but "we just weren't able to pull them off right now."385 The poster is the festival's whole pitch on one sheet, and it gets argued over, reworked and, now and then, thrown out the night before.

One man's inbox

Coachella's lineup is not a chart printout. Tollett, who co-founded the festival and still runs it as owner-operator in partnership with AEG, told the LA Times in 2019 that agents send him "another 3,000 or 4,000" acts a year on top of what he chases himself, and that he is "looking for 140 or 150 artists that are fresh."24 That year's anniversary bill came to 160 acts.24

"Fresh" is the point. He wants something unique for what he calls a "100-day window" around the festival, and he books against acts that have just played nearby.24 Some of what comes in he waves off. When WME's Marc Geiger pitched Kate Bush, Tollett's answer, as The New Yorker told it in 2017, was "No one is going to understand it."386

The same profile carried the clearest numbers ever published on what the bill costs. Tollett said each of the 2017 headliners - Radiohead, Lady Gaga and Kendrick Lamar - was paid about $3-4M, and the lowest-billed acts a little under $10,000.41 That is a gap of at least 300 times between the top line and the bottom line of the same poster. Pay is only part of what an agent is fighting over.

Twenty percent of the job

An agent negotiating a Coachella slot is negotiating four things at once: the fee, the stage, the time slot and the billing - where the name sits on the poster and how big it is. Headliners can demand top billing more than a year ahead.387 Wasserman's Lee Anderson told The Ringer that billing is "probably 20 percent of what I do for a living."387

The reason is that the poster reads like a ranking. The format has barely changed since 2003: names in rows, with the type shrinking line by line to show where an act stands.388 Agents bargain over that hard, and some call it one of the most stressful parts of booking a festival.388

The size is Goldenvoice's guess at an act's draw, and the guess can be wrong. 2 Chainz was billed small in 2013 and drew more people than his stage could hold.389 Tollett himself told The New Yorker that streaming has made it harder to tell whether an act belongs on the second line or the third.386

And sometimes the act reads the poster too. In 2019 Burna Boy, on the third tier, went on Instagram Live: "I am an AFRICAN GIANT and will not be reduced to whatever that tiny writing means."276 The Ringer cites his complaint as a case of an artist going public about type size.387 What he did next is in the chapter on what Coachella started.

Eurostile, and the one exception

Every name on the modern poster is set in one typeface, Eurostile LT, a wide, squared-off sans that looks like the label on a 1970s stereo.390 Its sameness is the point. If everyone gets the same letters, the only thing left to compare is size.

Goldenvoice broke the rule once. On the poster for 2025, "The Original Misfits" appeared in the band's own horror-movie lettering, dripping and jagged among the clean Eurostile names.390 Tollett's reason was history, not hype: "The Misfits are one of the first bands we promoted."390 The font was a thank-you note to one of Goldenvoice's own first bookings.

The lore · The font-size Hunger Games

Fans read the poster as a pay chart. Stereogum's 2014 headline called the scramble the "font-size Hunger Games."389 The documented version is less tidy: the size is Goldenvoice's estimate of an act's draw, bargained over alongside fee, stage and slot, and it is not the fee itself.389387 Fans have a second ritual too, the fake posters that circulate every winter before the real one.391

When the poster drops

For most of the festival's life the lineup came out in January. Music Festival Wizard's tally puts every drop from 2005 to 2012 somewhere between Jan. 9 and Jan. 31; that list is a single undated page, and at least one of its weekdays is wrong, so treat those early dates as approximate.392 From 2013 the dates are firmer, and they kept creeping earlier into the new year until the drop settled on the first days of January.393

Then the calendar broke. The 2025 lineup came out Nov. 20, 2024, about two months ahead of the old schedule.394 The 2026 lineup came out Sept. 15, 2025, about four months early.68 And for 2027 Goldenvoice skipped the poster altogether, at least at first: the dates (Apr. 9-11 and 16-18) were confirmed in April 2026 and advance passes went on sale before any names were announced.395 That sale opened May 1, 2026, with weekend 1 GA from $599 and a $49 down payment.396 As of Oct. 30, 2026, there was still no official 2027 lineup. TMZ reported SZA, Dua Lipa and Tame Impala as headliners, and Consequence wrote that the announcement was "expected soon."397

By the numbers · When the lineup came out

  • 2014: Jan. 8, 2014393
  • 2015: Jan. 6, 2015393
  • 2016: Jan. 4, 2016393
  • 2017: Jan. 3, 2017393
  • 2018: Jan. 2, 2018393
  • 2019: Jan. 2, 2019384
  • 2020: Jan. 2, 2020 (the festival was later cancelled)398
  • 2021: no lineup (no festival)393
  • 2022: Jan. 12, 2022393
  • 2023: Jan. 10, 2023399
  • 2024: Jan. 16, 2024400
  • 2025: Nov. 20, 2024394
  • 2026: Sept. 15, 202568
  • 2027: passes on sale May 1, 2026; no lineup as of Oct. 30, 2026396397

Why the drop moved

Goldenvoice has not said publicly why it moved the date, so everything here is the industry reading the tea leaves. The obvious context is 2024. Tickets for that year went on sale Jan. 19 and were still available a week later, as SFGate reported; Complex called it the "slowest ticket sales in ten years."401 Weekend 1 sold out about a month in.401 Weekend 2 never did.68 For a festival whose 2015 passes had reportedly gone in 40 minutes, that was a long month.401

The next on-sale did not fully fix it. Days after the Nov. 22, 2024 presale for 2025, weekend 1 GA was mostly gone, but weekend 2 GA was "widely available." Digital Music News noted that "for decades" Coachella had sold out the day tickets went on sale.402

Then the fall drop worked. The 2026 bill - Sabrina Carpenter, Justin Bieber and Karol G, with Anyma in a special slot - came out in mid-September, and both weekends sold out in about three days.53 By Pollstar's count it was the first time three pop acts had headlined, and Karol G was the first Latina headliner.68 Pollstar asked people in the business why. WME's Josh Kurfirst said "announcing early allows them to capture the hype around fall tours currently playing out."68 A venue owner, Stephen Chilton, offered a blunter guess: "they are trying to get out an onsale while things are as good as they are."68

The radius clause

The other reason the poster matters is what signing it costs an artist. Court filings in 2018 laid out the terms of Coachella's radius clause, the contract rule that limits where a booked act can play.403 According to Rolling Stone's reading of the filings, an act playing Coachella could not play any other North American festival from Dec. 15 to May 1. It could not play shows in Los Angeles, Orange, Riverside, San Bernardino, Santa Barbara, Ventura or San Diego counties in that window.
