It could not promote another U.S. festival until Coachella's lineup was out. And it could not "advertise, publicize or leak" a show in a way that interfered with Coachella's marketing.403

Tollett defends it as ordinary. He compared it to car dealerships, each with its own region, and pointed out that artists usually come back to the area later in the year.24 In Tollett's telling, that 100-day window is the clause's whole purpose: for that stretch, the acts on the poster are something you can only see in Indio.24

In April 2018, Soul'd Out Productions, which ran a festival in Portland, Ore., sued Goldenvoice and AEG in federal court there. It said the clause propped up "an illegal monopoly," that SZA and Daniel Caesar had turned it down because of it, and that Goldenvoice waived the clause for its own festivals but not for rivals. Goldenvoice said it would "vigorously defend."404

The case took two and a half years. In March 2019 Judge Michael Mosman dismissed it with prejudice, finding no antitrust injury.405 In May 2020 the Ninth Circuit reversed and sent it back,406 and in August Mosman let it go to discovery.407 AEG's answer admitted the clause exists and raised 16 defenses, among them that the amount in dispute was under $75,000.408 In November 2020 the two sides settled. The case was dismissed with prejudice, "each party to bear its own attorneys' fees," and no terms were disclosed.409 No court ever decided whether the clause itself was legal.

The leaks

The radius clause says artists may not leak. In practice the biggest names keep giving themselves away. Rage Against the Machine announced 2020 reunion dates in November 2019 that more or less confirmed their headline slot, and Consequence named the 2020 headliners three days before the poster - though its first version wrongly included My Chemical Romance.410 In November 2024, Post Malone's own tour announcement revealed his Coachella headline a day before the poster.411

The ones who said no

The top line is also the hardest to fill, and the misses only surface as reports. For 2025, Digital Music News reported that Kendrick Lamar and Rihanna had declined headline offers.402 For 2027 the rumor cycle started in September 2026 with HITS, which named Tame Impala and Dua Lipa,412 and by late October TMZ was reporting that BTS and Rage Against the Machine had turned the festival down.397 None of that is confirmed by Goldenvoice, and none of it has to be. A festival that sells its passes before the poster is out has already been paid for the suspense.

When the top line falls out

The top line is the line that most often changes. Beyoncé withdrew from 2017 on her doctor's orders during her pregnancy. Lady Gaga took her slot, and Beyoncé was announced for 2018.413414 Justin Timberlake dropped out of 2019 weeks before the announcement, after a vocal cord injury disrupted his tour.24

Then came West. According to Billboard's report, relayed by Digital Music News, his team asked on New Year's Day for a huge dome in the middle of the grounds, designed and supervised by John McGuire, to be ready by the April 12 start. Goldenvoice said it could not be built in four months; it would also have meant moving a large portable-toilet area tied into sewer and water lines. West's reported answer was no dome, no show.161 Within about 24 hours, the same report says, Ariana Grande had the slot.161 Tollett confirmed to the LA Times that the poster with West on it was in production until Jan. 1.24 West did come to Coachella that year, on Easter Sunday, for Sunday Service on a hill built between weekends.365 That story is in the camping chapter.

The dome would have stood about where the toilets were. Every poster is a set of decisions like that one, made months early and quietly undone when they don't work out.

Still open

  • Goldenvoice's own reason for the September drop. Two Billboard explainers that may contain it would not load for this chapter.
  • The pre-2013 drop dates, which rest on one undated list.
  • Whether the radius clause still reads the way the 2018 filings say it did. The settlement kept the current terms private.

Chapter 12

12The build, the art, the ticket, the food

The short versionCoachella is built from an empty polo field in about five weeks, and taken down again in time for Stagecoach. That means a PA crew that has done it for nearly two decades, a tent standing on 1,500 tons of concrete, a curfew that bills by the minute, a livestream directed with no rehearsals, and an ex-Hollywood effects man who builds the biggest thing that can go up in ten days. Then there is the wristband that gets you in, most often bought on a payment plan, and a four-course dinner in a rose garden.

The 2024 Sahara tent sits on about 1,500 tons of concrete. Crews poured roughly 156 truckloads of it into underground footings starting in December 2023, working overnight so the trucks stayed off Indio's streets in daytime.83 The structure on top is steel, not the old aluminum, and 320 ft long by the Desert Sun's measure.83 Elation, which supplied 956 of its lights for the rig, gives the footprint as about 400 by 410 ft and nearly 100 ft tall, with four headliner-level acts a day and changeovers as short as 10-15 minutes.129 The Desert Sun's 320 ft and Elation's 400 by 410 ft don't agree, and neither says how it measured. That is the permanent part. Almost everything else on the grounds is put up from scratch each spring and taken away again within weeks.

Five weeks on a polo field

The clearest look at the build calendar comes from a job ad. AEG's stagehand posting for 2025 asked applicants to be available from Mar. 17 to May 9 and gave the expected work period as Mar. 30 to May 3.91 Duties were staging, truck loading, scenery rigging, and helping with audio, video and lighting, on day, mid and overnight shifts.91 By the dates, that window takes in both Coachella weekends and Stagecoach, which uses the same grounds right after.

The crews also have to talk to each other across a site that big. For the two festivals in 2025, Clear-Com's intercom covered 12 stages over three weeks, with 61 wireless beltpacks and 23 networked panels.415

Specialist crews arrive in waves. Rat Sound, the PA company, starts prepping gear about two weeks out, and its trucks start rolling onto the site about a week before doors.126 The art installers get about 11 days.416

Some of it gets built at a speed nobody planned for. Between the two weekends of 2019, crews built a grass hill from nothing for Kanye West's Easter Sunday Service: dirt went in on Tuesday, sod on Thursday and Friday.365 The hidden speakers in it were designed by Perry Tollett, the festival chief's brother.365

The work is dangerous. On Apr. 6, 2019, a Goldenvoice lead rigger who had spent 20 years on the Coachella team died in a fall during stage setup. Indio police called it an industrial accident, and OSHA led the investigation.417

When it is over, the site flips. For Stagecoach, the main stage is rotated 90 degrees, the Mojave becomes the Palomino, the Sahara and the VIP rose garden go unused, and most of the large art leaves.84 The Ferris wheel stays put.84

The PA

Rat Sound has done the audio for almost every Coachella there has been - 18 of the first 19, as of 2018.126 That year the system was mostly L-Acoustics: about 847 main cabinets across the site, not counting sidefills and wedges, with 168 K2 cabinets on the main stage field alone.126 Rat Sound put 65-70 people on the festival and rented 25-30 local houses to sleep them.126

The desert has its own rules for sound. In high wind, and at night, the PA hangs and delay clusters are lowered and tied off.12689 No published decibel limit for the festival turned up in the research for this chapter; the limit that is published, and enforced in dollars, is the clock.

The curfew meter

Coachella has to stop at 1 a.m. on Friday and Saturday, and midnight on Sunday.72 Run past it and Goldenvoice pays the city: $20,000 once a set goes five minutes over, then $1,000 for every minute after that, into Indio's general fund.72

The meter runs most years. In 2023 weekend 1 cost $117,000, with sets running long every night; Frank Ocean, who arrived late, went 25 minutes over.72 "Guys I'm being told it's curfew, so that's the end of the show," he told the crowd.72 On weekend 2 of 2026, TMZ put the bill at $44,000: $20,000 for Justin Bieber's two minutes and $24,000 for Anyma's nine.418 Those numbers suggest the $20,000 covers any overrun up to five minutes, with the per-minute charge after that. The same report has Paul McCartney running 54 minutes over in 2009 for $54,000, which predates the current schedule.418 The pyramid chapter has the other famous overrun.

Power

The grounds sit in the territory of the Imperial Irrigation District, the public utility that powers Indio, not Southern California Edison.419 How much of the festival runs off IID's grid, and how much runs off generators, has never been published. The festival's own sustainability page lists what it does in general terms: renewable diesel for generators, on-site solar panels, "available utility energy" and "hybridized power."140 One stage is specific. Since 2025 the Do LaB has run on "zero-emissions battery systems."140

The headliners bring more. PRG supplied power distribution as well as lighting for J Balvin's main-stage set in 2024.420

The same page says about 35,000 people camp on site, that the festival doubled its water spigots, and that Coachella and Stagecoach together composted or recycled 616,278 pounds of waste in 2024.140

The livestream machine

In 2010 Coachella's webcast showed one act at a time. In 2011, YouTube streamed three stages on separate channels to nearly 4 million people.265 By 2026 the stream covered seven stages plus a 24/7 "Coachella Station."421

It is produced by Springboard Productions, led by Hank Neuberger, who has produced Coachella's live content since 2007.422 In 2024 the operation used more than 200 crew, six video trucks and one director per stage, cutting live sets with no rehearsal.422 Coverage had moved from sports-broadcast cameras to cinema cameras and lenses, and each stage got its own broadcast mix.422 One vendor, Media Stream Wave, says it supplied two trucks and 40 Blackmagic cameras for screens and broadcast across all eight stages that year.423

Directing blind is the job. No rehearsal means the director meets the show at the same time as the viewers do.

In September 2026 Goldenvoice and YouTube extended their exclusive deal through 2030, covering both weekends. Terms were not disclosed. YouTube said the 2026 stream set its record for concurrent viewers, without giving a number.267

Cell towers on wheels

A field full of phones will swamp an ordinary network, so the carriers drive in their own. A COW is a cell on wheels: a mobile tower on a trailer. In 2013 AT&T ran an 18-beam antenna and at least three COWs, peaking at about 83 GB an hour and moving 6,054 GB over the two weekends.424 By 2016 it had a "Drum Set" antenna and four COWs, 59 beams in all, and its customers used 29.2 TB.425 In 2026 T-Mobile brought "multiple" COWs and offered satellite texting as a fallback.426

The art

Paul Clemente has run Coachella's art since 2007. Before that he spent 18 years in Hollywood effects, on miniatures and rigging for Titanic and The Matrix, and he now leads the festival's own fabricators.87 His rule, as PBS SoCal's Artbound put it in 2013, is the "largest, most dramatic" pieces that can be built in ten days.427 The festival pays for the work in full.427

"We have this incredible venue like no one else in the country has, usually with zero possibility of rain." - Paul Clemente87

The pitch process has changed. In 2013 Goldenvoice got about 300 proposals, a record.427 By 2016 it was picking six or seven pieces from as many as 250 and building almost all of them itself, with local firms and an on-site shop.416 Today the team mostly approaches artists directly, and most large work is built in a 20,000 sq ft tent near the grounds.87 Since 2024 the program has been curated with Public Art Company, founded by Raffi Lehrer, a former Goldenvoice associate art director.428429

The lore · The Burning Man hand-me-downs

The legend goes that Coachella's art is just Burning Man's, driven a few hours south. For the early years, that is close to right: LA Magazine reports that the first pieces were borrowed from Burning Man.87 The snail started there too, as a concept in 2002.427 What changed is that Coachella started paying. It moved to open calls, then to approaching artists directly, and it funds the selected work in full and builds most of it on site.87427416

The snail and the astronaut

In 2013 Poetic Kinetics brought Helix Poeticus: a snail about 30 ft tall and 80 ft long, with iridescent silver skin, built on top of a variable-reach forklift - the same kind of machine that builds the festival.427 It moved.

In 2014 the same studio brought Escape Velocity, an astronaut 36 ft tall made of steel tubing, chicken wire and pin rods, which drifted through the crowd on a forklift of its own.430 It "was to be sold" after the festival.430 In 2019 the astronaut came back, crouched at about 45 ft as Overview Effect, its suit looking "burnt from reentry."431

Spectra, which stayed

Spectra, a seven-story spiral tower with an observation deck by Patrick O'Mahony's studio Newsubstance, was commissioned in 2018.87 It was built in the UK, was meant to come down after 2020, and stayed, through Covid delays and because people liked it.87 The studio now calls it a "permanent feature" and lists appearances in Fortnite, Minecraft and Family Guy.432 Whether it is still standing in 2026 is less clear: ArchDaily's 2026 list of permanent works leaves it off.433

The wheel

The Ferris wheel is not Goldenvoice's. It belongs to Ray Cammack Shows, a Phoenix-area carnival family in business since 1963.85 The current wheel, the La Grande XL, replaced the original in 2017. According to the company's Ben Pickett, it arrives on 20 semis and goes up in five days with a 70-ton crane and a 10-person crew, with 36 air-conditioned gondolas.86 Phoenix New Times, in an undated piece, gives a different build: a 60-ton crane and 10 days.85 Either way, it is one of the few big things that stays standing for Stagecoach.84

Where the pieces go

Not all of it leaves. ArchDaily's 2026 list of works that now stay on the grounds includes Sarbalé ke, Etherea, Colossal Cacti, The Messengers, Monarchs and Mutts.433 Francis Kéré's Sarbalé ke has also gone the other way, into a park in Indio, and newer pieces are designed in modules so they can live on as public art somewhere else.434 The signs get reused by EcoSet.435

One 2016 piece was about the people next door. The Date Farmers' Sneaking into the Show was about Coachella Valley locals who live beside the festival but can't get in.416

The ticket

A Coachella day cost $50 in 1999.3 Weekend 1 GA for 2026 started at $649.46

By the numbers · What GA cost

  • 1999: $50 a day3
  • 2004: $142 for two days27
  • 2007: $249 for three days28
  • 2009: $269 for three days436
  • 2012: $285 a weekend, plus fees (the first two-weekend year)42
  • 2013: $34943
  • 2018: $42944
  • 2022: $449-549 before fees, tiered437
  • 2025: $599 weekend 1, $549 weekend 2, at the presale402
  • 2026: from $649 weekend 1, $549 weekend 246
  • 2027: weekend 1 tiers from $599 to $699438

Most people do not pay it at once. Coachella introduced payment plans in 2009, during the recession, against what was described as sluggish sales. That year 18-19% of buyers used one.439436 By 2025 about 60% did, according to Billboard's figure as reported by Entrepreneur, with $49.99 down, three installments and no interest.301 Miss a payment by more than 10 days and the order is cancelled automatically, and you get a credit good for a year.301

The tickets are sold by AXS, which is owned by AEG, the same company behind Goldenvoice.440 Before that, in 2014, the official seller was Front Gate Tickets.377

What you get is a wristband with an RFID chip. Intellitix's RFID access control was on Coachella by 2011.441 Now the band is registered once, in the Coachella app only, with a legal name and the six-letter code on the back. Two bands on one wrist block the scan, and passes from unauthorized sellers "can be canceled at any time." The official resale is AXS's own.442

That cancel button is also the weak spot. A seller can resell a band, report it lost or stolen, and have it switched off. CV Independent described the scam in 2014.377 In 2026 one buyer paid more than $2,000 for three bands on OfferUp that worked on day one and were dead on day two.443 The camping chapter has the rest of Scamchella.

Food and drink

Roy Choi's Kogi truck came to Coachella in 2009. In Choi's memory, festival food back then was "a world of sliced pizzas and sugary lemonade." His truck broke down and showed up late, and still, he says, "just instantly connected."310 Goldenvoice wanted Choi back, and started adding other chef-driven vendors.310 By 2017 culinary curator Nic Adler was booking more than 150 restaurants and vendors, over 40 of them curated, and pizza was $7 a slice.444445 In 2026 culinary director Adrian Garcia ran "over 100 bars and restaurants," including a Nobu omakase inside the Red Bull Mirage.446

Dinner in the Rose Garden

The VIP Rose Garden is the old polo club's garden, and every night of both weekends it seats a long-table dinner by Outstanding in the Field, the land artist Jim Denevan's traveling dinner series. It is about 200 guests a night, served family style: a welcome cocktail, four courses, Rose Garden access, and a festival pass required on top.81

It cost $225 in 2015 and 2016,311312 and $350 by 2023.447 The start date is muddy. LA Weekly covered it in 2015, while Flaunt billed 2026 as its 11th year, which would make the first one 2016.311446

Beer, and then beer anywhere

The Craft Beer Barn opened in 2014, when Adler asked Tony Yanow, of the LA bars Tony's Darts Away and Mohawk Bend, to build it: 120-150 beers on up to 40 taps, cash only.78 By 2017 it poured from 100-150 breweries and had a rare-beer bar inside.444 At Stagecoach the same barn is renamed for Guy Fieri.84

For most of the festival's life, drinking was fenced. Drinkers "were always confined to the beer gardens or craft beer areas," and couldn't carry a drink back onto the field.74 2023 was a soft change. 2024 was the first clear year of walk-around drinking, with IDs checked and 21+ wristbands handed out; the gardens stayed, but the guards at the exits stopped turning drinks back.74 The Independent reported that bars were spread across the grounds after complaints from people who had the wristband.75 A 16-oz Heineken that year was $15.74

Prices since then are mostly what attendees post. In 2026, as reported by attendees and collected by FOODbible, that meant a $24 Red Bull vodka, a $17 coffee and a $23 burrito.448

Still open

  • How much power the festival draws, and how much of it comes from IID's grid against generators. Nothing has been published.
  • How many people build it. The stagehand posting gives the dates, not a headcount.
  • Whether a decibel limit exists in the Indio agreement, and if so what it is.
  • Whether Spectra still stands.

Chapter 13

13The money and Indio

The short versionThe last published gross is $114.6M, for 2017. Since then the festival's own numbers have gone quiet, and the figures that do get printed come from the city, the tourism bureau or the governor's office, none of them an independent audit. Indio settled for a few dollars a ticket after a 2012 standoff, and in 2024 that came to just over $2M. The people who clean the field overnight are often the same people who pick the valley's grapes.

In 2024, the city of Indio collected $2,000,206.13 from three weekends of festivals on the polo field.55 Seven years earlier, the festival on that field grossed $114.6M on its own.38 Those are the two hardest numbers in this chapter. Almost everything else is an estimate, and it matters who made it.

What the festival grosses

A gross is total ticket sales, the figure Billboard Boxscore and Pollstar chart from box office reports. For Coachella those charts run through 2017, and the figures climb every year:

By the numbers · Reported grosses

  • 2013: $67.2M35
  • 2014: $78.3M35
  • 2015: $84.3M, on 198,000 tickets36
  • 2016: $94.2M, on 198,000 tickets37
  • 2017: $114.6M, on 250,000 tickets38

2017 made Coachella the first recurring festival to gross over $100M.39 Then the numbers stop. This project found no Boxscore or Pollstar gross for any year after 2017. By 2019, The Ringer could say only that the yearly gross was "well over $100 million."3 Nobody outside the company has published a better number since.

The years before 2013 are murkier. Wikipedia's table, which cites Boxscore, puts 2011 at about $25M and the first two-weekend year, 2012, at about $47.3M.18 If those hold, the second weekend nearly doubled the gross in a single year, which is roughly what you'd expect from selling the same show twice.

Selling the same show twice

The biggest financial decision in the festival's history was announced in a sentence: from 2012, a second weekend, same lineup, separate tickets.32 Tollett's reasons, as he gave them in 2011, were about scarcity. Demand was already past what the 2011 site could hold. Adding about 40,000 people a day would "ruin the show." And resellers were getting $500-600 for passes, which he called unfair.32

So instead of a bigger festival, he cloned it. The same artists play the same stages a week apart. Every gross in the panel above is two weekends' worth.

Goldenvoice did the same sum again in October 2016 with Desert Trip, six classic-rock acts over two weekends on the same field. Tollett told The New York Times it would gross "approximately $160 million for the two weekends."115 Stereogum set that against $84M for Coachella, which is the 2015 figure; Pollstar's chart has Coachella 2016 at $94.2M.11437 Either way, two weekends of the Stones, Dylan and McCartney out-grossed the festival. Stereogum put the cost of staging it at about $100M.114

Some sense of where the money goes came from Paul Tollett himself. He told The New Yorker in 2017 that each of that year's headliners was paid $3-4M, and that acts at the bottom of the bill got a little under $10,000.41 For 2026, Rolling Stone reported that Justin Bieber's first headline set paid "north of $10 million." That figure came from one unnamed source.449

Who owns it

Goldenvoice runs Coachella. Goldenvoice is owned by AEG, the Anschutz Entertainment Group. AEG bought Goldenvoice in 2001.450 Wikipedia puts the price at $7 million; this project hasn't found that figure in the original reporting, so treat it as Wikipedia's.18 Coachella itself came later. Tollett sold AEG half of the festival in 2004, the year it first sold out.3

The Anschutz donations

In January 2017, The FADER reported what the Anschutz Foundation had given to. Between 2010 and 2013 it gave $190,000 combined to Alliance Defending Freedom, the National Christian Foundation and the Family Research Council, groups The FADER described as anti-LGBTQ.451 In the 2016 election cycle, Philip and Nancy Anschutz gave $1,012,000 to Republican candidates and PACs, including $500,000 to the Senate Leadership Fund.451

Anschutz called the claims that he is anti-LGBTQ "fake news." He said he and the foundation stopped giving to groups once they learned those groups backed anti-LGBTQ causes. He did not deny the donations.451

Tollett, asked about it by The New Yorker that spring, did not defend the politics. He was blunt about the position he was in:

"I was offended. I run the festival, but it's rude to say that when you're a partner with someone."20

The festival went ahead that April as planned, and grossed its record $114.6M.38

Indio's cut: the standoff

In 2012, Indio was short of money. The city had just cut about $4M through layoffs and furloughs.65 So the council looked at the biggest event in town and floated an admissions tax: 5-10% on any event over 2,500 people. Goldenvoice worked it out at about $36 a ticket, or $4-6M a year.65

Tollett's answer was to threaten to leave. He said the 2013 festivals could be the last in Indio, that Goldenvoice would skip 2014 and find a new site for 2015.65 He also listed what the festival already paid: more than $800,000 in ticket fees, sales tax on about $9M of food and drink, and more than $800,000 in hotel tax.65

At the time, the city's per-ticket share was $2.33. That came to about $500,000 a year, in a city budget of about $49M.452 Councilman Sam Torres had pushed for a 6% tax.

"I looked at these guys as a way to help us continue to maintain our city."452

The 2013 deal

The tax never passed. In April 2013 the council voted 4-0 for a new agreement instead, with Mayor Elaine Holmes abstaining over a property conflict.66 The terms:

  • Coachella and Stagecoach stay in Indio through at least 2030.
  • The city's share rises from $2.33 to $5.01 a ticket, starting in 2014.
  • The site can go from three festivals a year to five.66453

NPR's summary was "$5 a ticket," or about a million dollars a year.454 The five-festival clause is the one that later made room for Desert Trip and Power Trip in October (see the next chapter).

In 2021 the deal was stretched to 2050. The council voted 3-1, with Holmes recusing because she owns property within 1,000 feet of the site.56 Councilmember Glenn Miller made the case for it:

"Not one dollar comes out of the city of Indio's pocket."56

The same year, Goldenvoice signed a lease with the Haagen family that runs to 2050 and gives it the polo club year-round, with room for two more large festivals a year.14455

Indio's cut: the 2024 numbers

For 2024, a city spokesperson gave KESQ the details. Indio counted 81,690 tickets for Coachella's first weekend, 80,148 for the second and 89,003 for Stagecoach, 250,841 in all. It collected $2,000,206.13, at a rate of "$2.658 per ticket/per day this fiscal year."55

The city's arithmetic checks out exactly: 250,841 tickets, times three days, times $2.658, is $2,000,206. That works out to about $7.97 per three-day ticket. That is our arithmetic, not the city's. It is well above the $5.01 of the 2013 deal, and the public record found for this piece doesn't say how the rate got there or when it switched to a per-day basis. The amended agreements would.

Notice the order of those ticket counts. Stagecoach, the country festival the weekend after, sold more tickets than either Coachella weekend that year.55

The city also gets paid when the music runs late. The curfew carries a $20,000 fine once a set goes five minutes over, then $1,000 a minute, and the money goes into Indio's general fund.72 For the first weekend of 2023, the city fined Goldenvoice $117,000.456

By the numbers · What Indio gets

  • 1999: $90,000 fronted by the city for services, per Wikipedia18
  • 2012: $2.33 a ticket, about $500,000 a year452
  • 2014 on: $5.01 a ticket under the 2013 deal66
  • 2024: $2.658 per ticket per day, $2,000,206.13 in all55
  • 2023: $117,000 in curfew fines, first weekend alone456

What none of these numbers say is what share of Indio's budget the festivals cover today. The 2013 figure, about $500,000 against $49M, is about 1%.452 No current equivalent turned up.

The impact numbers, and who made them

Every spring, a big number goes around: what Coachella is "worth" to the valley. It has been $254M, $600M, $700M and $1.4B. Here is where each one came from.

  • $254.4M (2012). An economic-impact analysis said Coachella brought that much to the region, with more than a third of it landing in Indio.453 Hypebot credits the analysis to Development Management Group, which put Indio's share at $89.2M.66 NPR described a study putting the festival's Indio impact at more than $90M as "Goldenvoice-commissioned."454
  • $1.4B (2016). A projection by Michael Bracken of Development Management Group, of Palm Desert, for all five Goldenvoice festivals that year, Desert Trip included. It put $805M in the Coachella Valley and about $212M in Indio, and was announced by the valley's tourism bureau and its economic partnership. NBC Los Angeles noted that Bracken's relationship with AEG and Goldenvoice was "undisclosed and unclear."457
  • About $700M lost (2020). When the festivals were cancelled, the Coachella Valley Economic Partnership estimated the valley would lose about $700M.458
  • About $600M (2024). NBC Palm Springs credited this one to the City of Indio. No study was named.459
  • More than $700M a year (2026). Gov. Gavin Newsom's office said Coachella and Stagecoach together generate that, plus more than 10,000 temporary jobs.460 NBC Palm Springs added a figure of more than $100M a year in value to Indio.461 Neither report named the study behind it.

None of these is an independent audit. Two come from the same consultant, at least one was paid for by the promoter, and the recent ones cite no method at all. They may all be roughly right. A valley that fills its hotels for three weekends running is clearly getting something. But the figures tell you more about who wanted to say a number than about the number.

The lore · A billion-dollar festival

The legend goes that Coachella is a billion-dollar event for the desert, and you'll see "$1.4 billion" quoted as if it were one festival's yearly haul. That figure is real, but it is a 2016 projection, by a consultant whose ties to the promoter were never disclosed, for five festivals at once, worldwide.457 Indio's share in that same projection was about $212M. Nobody has measured it since.

The rooms

The clearest independent signal is the hotels. Expedia's data showed Coachella Valley hotels more than 90% full in April, the most expensive month of the year, with the average nightly rate climbing from $280 in April 2016 to $340 in April 2017.382 "April always leads the entire year for Coachella Valley hotels," the report said.382

Short-term rentals are where the stories get wild. Homeowners and agents told NBC Palm Springs in 2026 that some luxury homes rent for more than $100,000 for a single weekend.383 That's anecdote, not data. Nobody published the bookings.

Who does the work

The festival's labor is a mix of stagehands, private security, temporary crews and, at night, farmworkers.

The overnight cleanup. In 2019, The Desert Sun's Rebecca Plevin followed grape workers who take overnight trash shifts at the polo grounds across all three April weekends. They finish around 3:30 a.m., after about eight hours in the vineyards the day before, and are paid minimum wage.462 The reason more of them need the shifts is in the fields: the county counted about 1,000 fewer acres of grapes in 2017 than in 2013.462 Enriqueta Magaña, a supervisor with 20 years in the vineyards and 10 at the festival, described the grounds the morning after as "a garbage dump."462

The Marines. In 2022, the San Francisco Standard reported that active-duty Marines, many from Camp Pendleton, had worked security shifts at Coachella for C.I. Security Specialists. In exchange, the company donated toward Marine Corps birthday-ball tickets. One Marine estimated that about half of roughly 200 of the company's officers there were volunteer Marines.463 The company said its personnel were all paid employees. State regulators said the Marines needed guard licenses, and two of those interviewed had none.463

The guards. By 2025, close to 1,000 Allied Universal guards were posted around the clock inside the gates, according to NBC Palm Springs.370

The build. In April 2019, lead rigger Christopher Griffin, 49, a 20-year Goldenvoice employee, died after falling from scaffolding during setup.464 In 2025, a former stagehand filed a wage suit against the companies behind the festival, alleging that hourly workers drive up to three hours to job sites without being paid for the travel.465

The guests, sometimes. In 2019, the rapper Belly sued Coachella, Goldenvoice and the security firm IPS, saying up to 25 guards attacked him during the Weeknd's set the year before.466

Selling it: the slump and the snap-back

For most of the 2010s, selling out was the easy part. Then it wasn't.

In 2024, the first weekend took nearly a month to sell out. About 80% of the year's 250,000 tickets had sold by opening day, and sales were down 14-17% from the year before, according to Billboard's sources.45 The second weekend never fully sold out.68

2025 was split. First-weekend general admission was gone by December 2024; second-weekend passes were "widely available."467 By April, second-weekend passes were reselling on StubHub for as little as $246, less than half of face value.52

Inside the fence, the prices kept their own pace. In 2025, Digital Music News priced three beers at $45 and two tacos at $16, and hotel packages from about $3,300 to more than $9,000.467

Goldenvoice's response was to move the calendar. The 2026 lineup came out on September 15, 2025, months earlier than usual.68 Both weekends' GA and VIP passes sold out three days after going on sale.53 The chapter on the lineup covers why an early poster changes the math.

The brands

Hypebeast dates the first big branded spaces on the grounds to Heineken in 2006, followed by H&M, American Express and Absolut.287 When the festival split into two weekends in 2012, the brand parties off-site mostly stuck to the first one.288

The off-site houses peaked and then thinned out. By 2019, Glossy reported Sephora, H&M, Urban Decay and others scaling back or dropping their VIP experiences. Conor Begley of Tribe Dynamics:

"There's definitely been a decline in activations, especially larger-branded experiences."292

The money moved inside the fence instead. SponsorUnited counted the 2026 on-site sponsors by number of branded assets: Heineken led with 23, then Aperol with 19 and Gap with 14. Heineken's cans lit up when they touched, Gap built a "Hoodie House," Pinterest ran a phone-free zone, and HBO screened the Euphoria season three premiere in the campgrounds. No sponsorship fees were disclosed.468

The company with the most riding on the festival doesn't sponsor it at all. Revolve, the online fashion retailer, "typically earns about 30% of its yearly revenue in the weeks around Coachella," Retail Brew reported in 2020, citing New York Times reporting. When the 2020 festival was postponed, Revolve's festival-style orders dropped to about a fifth of normal.291 Revolve's own party, and what happened to it, is in the next chapter.

Still open

  • The gross after 2017. If you have a Boxscore or Pollstar figure for any Coachella since, we'd like to see it.
  • How $5.01 became $2.658 a day. The amended Indio agreements would show when the rate changed and why.
  • Indio's share now. What fraction of the city's general fund the festivals cover in a current budget.
  • An independent impact study. One that isn't the promoter's, the city's or the tourism bureau's.

Chapter 14

14The valley and the season

The short versionCoachella is two weekends in a longer desert season. The same polo field hosts a country festival the week after, which outsold it in 2024, and has held classic-rock and metal festivals in October. An art biennial puts billboards on the road into Palm Springs, parties fill hangars and horse parks, and pool parties take over in August. The festival is named for a valley and held in Indio, while the city actually called Coachella is its own place, in a part of the valley the festival rarely touches.

In the spring of 2017, the drive into Palm Springs had billboards that showed you the mountains they were blocking. Jennifer Bolande put photographs of the ranges ahead on a run of boards along Gene Autry Trail, each one placed so its horizon lined up with the real one at a single point as you drove toward it.469 They were not advertising Coachella. They were part of Desert X, an art show, and they stood through festival season.

That is the valley in April: the festival is the loudest thing in it, but not the only thing.

Stagecoach, the week after

By January 2007, Goldenvoice had a country festival on the calendar. The first Stagecoach ran May 5-6, 2007, at the Empire Polo Field, the week after Coachella, with George Strait and Kenny Chesney on top and Willie Nelson, Emmylou Harris and Brooks & Dunn on the bill. Goldenvoice co-produced it with The Messina Group and Moore Entertainment Group.470

It has grown up on the same grass. In 2016, Indio raised its cap from 75,000 to 85,000 a day, alongside Coachella's raise to 125,000.54 In 2025, the state alcohol regulator counted daily peaks of about 82,000 at Stagecoach, against nearly 110,000 at Coachella.69

And sometimes it wins. For 2024, Indio counted 89,003 Stagecoach tickets. Coachella's first weekend had 81,690 and its second 80,148.55 That was the year Coachella's sales sagged, but the country crowd was the biggest of the three weekends.

The changeover starts as Coachella's Sunday crowd leaves and runs through the week, until the grounds are a country-western venue.88 The grounds chapter has the full list of what gets renamed. What doesn't change is the weather.

On Saturday, April 25, 2026, at about 8 p.m., with gusts of 50-65 mph forecast and sustained winds of 25-35, Stagecoach was paused and the grounds cleared.90 The screens read:

"The festival has been postponed until further notice. Please move quickly and calmly to the nearest exit."90

It restarted at about 9:30 p.m. with a cheerier screen: "Back in the saddle." Journey and Riley Green were cancelled, Lainey Wilson moved to 10:30 p.m., and the night ran until 1 a.m.90471

The fall trips

The 2013 deal with Indio allowed up to five festivals a year on the site.66 For a while, Goldenvoice used the extra slots for something Coachella would never book as a whole lineup.

Desert Trip, October 2016. Two weekends of the Rolling Stones, Bob Dylan, Paul McCartney, Neil Young, Roger Waters and the Who. Stereogum's headline called it "Oldchella." Tickets ran from $199 for a single GA day to $1,599 for a reserved seat for the full weekend, with 150,000 people expected and 1,000 flushable toilets, against the usual 300.114 Tollett told The New York Times it would gross about $160M.115 The money chapter puts that next to Coachella's numbers.

Power Trip, October 6-8, 2023. The same idea, for metal. Iron Maiden and Guns N' Roses on Friday; Judas Priest, standing in for Ozzy Osbourne after he cancelled for health reasons, and AC/DC on Saturday; Tool and Metallica on Sunday. Up to 80,000 a day, with GA from $599 and the pit at $1,599.116

When Goldenvoice took over the polo club year-round in 2021, the lease allowed two more large three-day festivals a year on top of the spring ones.455

Desert X and the billboards

Desert X was meant to give the valley a different reason to visit. Its founder, Susan Davis, editorial director at Sunnylands, said in 2016 that people come for mid-century modern architecture, tennis, polo and music, but not for contemporary art.

"I'd like this exhibition to change that."472

It is produced by a nonprofit, the Desert Biennial, and puts site-specific installations by international artists across the valley.473 Four of its works have been billboards, three of them on one road: Gene Autry Trail, which runs from the I-10 into Palm Springs.

2017: Jennifer Bolande, Visible Distance / Second Sight. The landscape boards described above, at Gene Autry Trail and Vista Chino. The title nods to Burma-Shave, the shaving cream whose roadside signs were placed in sequence so the message only read from a moving car.469 She planned six double-sided boards and ended up with three, after complications with Lamar Advertising, whose inventory she used.474

2019: Cara Romero, Jackrabbit, Cottontail & Spirits of the Desert. Romero is a Chemehuevi photographer. Her five billboards showed enlarged photographs of four young Chemehuevi boys as time travelers returning to ancestral land. One stood in front of the Palm Springs wind turbines. Another showed all four in headdresses under the words "NO WALL."475 Desert X's map places the work in Palm Springs.476

2021: Xaviera Simmons, Because You Know Ultimately We Will Band a Militia. Billboards on Gene Autry Trail in plain ad typography, with lines like "California once tried to ban Black people" and "Rupture your guilt amnesia."477 Across the road stood a Rocky's Pawn Shop board reading "Make America Great" over the Stars and Stripes. PBS SoCal's writer wondered if the pairing was on purpose.477 That spring, Coachella was cancelled.51 Desert X went ahead.

2023: Tyre Nichols, Originals. Six photographs Nichols took in Memphis, among them the Hernando de Soto Bridge at night, a sunset and the Tom Lee monument, on billboards along North Gene Autry Trail.478 Nichols, a photographer originally from California, had been beaten to death by police in Memphis earlier that year.478 The work was supported by the City of Palm Springs Public Arts Commission and stayed up until May 7, through both Coachella weekends.479478

The schedule is about to change. Desert X's tenth-anniversary edition, curated by its artistic director Neville Wakefield and curator Kaitlin Garcia-Maestas, runs October 30, 2027 to May 7, 2028, opening in the fall instead of late winter.473 It runs into May 2028, so it should overlap the festival if 2028 keeps the usual April dates.

The billboard chapter covers the other kind of board on these roads, the paid ones.

The parties

Some of the most talked-about nights of Coachella week happen somewhere else.

Neon Carnival. Brent Bolthouse's party, run with Jeffrey Best, is invite-only and sits "near (but not affiliated with) Coachella," in TheWrap's phrasing. It starts after the headliners, runs to 4 a.m., books nightclub DJs, and in 2014 held about 2,000-3,000 wristbanded guests at a private airfield past Indio.480 Its age depends on who is counting. Bolthouse dated it to about 2007 in 2014.480 BizBash called the 2012 edition, in a Thermal airport hangar, the "third annual."288 By 2024 it was at the Desert International Horse Park in Thermal, for about 10,000 people, with Liquid I.V., Patrón El Alto, Levi's and Heineken as sponsors. You "can't buy" your way in, Bolthouse says.481

Revolve Festival. Revolve first flew influencers to Coachella in 2013.289 Its invite-only influencer event began in 2015, according to WWD, at the Avalon in Palm Springs; Business of Fashion dates it to 2016.290289 It peaked at about 5,000 guests in 2022.289 That year, at the Merv Griffin Estate in La Quinta, invited influencers waited two to three hours in the heat without water for shuttles, and the internet compared it to Fyre Festival.482 Creator Averie Bishop called it "a difficult and unsafe situation that could have been easily avoided."482 Revolve apologized, saying shuttle access was limited as the festival reached capacity.293 Two years later its festival was one day, with about 1,200 guests, and Revolve's marketing spend had fallen from $181M in 2022 to $171M in 2023.289

Pop-up events celebrating the festival still run across the valley on both weekends; NBC Palm Springs reported them again in 2026.483 But the big branded houses that used to line the circuit have mostly given way to activations inside the festival fence. The money chapter has that story.

Splash House, in August

Splash House, a weekend of DJs at Palm Springs hotel pools, was started by locals Kelly McLean and her brother Tyler. In August 2013 it ran across three hotels, the Saguaro, Caliente Tropics and The Curve, with shuttles between them.484 The Coachella Valley Independent's preview that year hoped it might "become yet another popular annual event."484

It did. In 2026 it ran two August weekends, August 7-9 and 14-16, at the Saguaro, the Hilton and the Renaissance, with after-hours sets at the Palm Springs Air Museum.485 It now appears on Goldenvoice's own festivals page.308

The count is slightly off. Visit Greater Palm Springs called 2026 the festival's 13th year, which would point to a 2014 start, while the 2013 preview describes an event that summer.485484 A skipped pandemic year would explain the gap, but nothing found here says so.

Coachella, the city

The festival is not in Coachella. It is named for the Coachella Valley and has always been held in Indio.4

The name itself is a mistake. In 1901, railroad man Jason Rector proposed "Conchilla," for the little fossil shells in the area, as the name of a new townsite. The printer set it as "Coachella," and the founders kept it rather than hold up the announcement. The valley took the name later, and the town became a city in 1946. The historian Jeff Crider told NPR the word means nothing in Spanish or English beyond the place itself.4

The city of Coachella is its own place, and 97.5% Latino.486 Its motto was once "The City of Eternal Sunshine." Type coachella.com and you get the festival.487

The place name and the trademark have met in court. In December 2021, a New Year's Eve event called "Coachella Day One 22" was planned at Coachella Crossroads, next to Spotlight 29 Casino in the city of Coachella, produced by the Twenty-Nine Palms Band of Mission Indians.488 The tribe has sovereign immunity, so Goldenvoice sued Live Nation, Ticketmaster and the web host instead.488 FOX 11 reported that a judge granted a temporary restraining order; Festival Insider later reported the order was denied and a preliminary injunction let the case go on.488489 The case settled in August 2022, terms undisclosed, and the tribe said the venue keeps the name Coachella Crossroads.489

The east valley

The valley's two big industries are agriculture, notably dates, and tourism and entertainment. The valley's first major farm labor strike was in 1965.4

In 2018, Gabriel Thompson reported on the east valley's farm towns for Type Investigations. Thermal, with about 2,396 people, and Mecca, with nearly 9,000, were each 99.9% Latino; Oasis was 98.2%. About a third of Thermal's residents lived below the poverty line, including nearly half its children. Drinking water on the east side contained arsenic, and there were illegal dumps. In grape season, some migrant workers slept in cars or parking lots.486 His summary of the festival's place in all this:

It "has little bearing on the lives of people on the valley's east side."486

Thermal is also where some festivalgoers land. In 2022, the flight company Blade expected to fly about 200 people from Santa Monica to Thermal for the festival, turning a five-hour drive into about 40 minutes.340

There is one point where the festival and the east valley's workers meet: the cleanup. Grape workers from these fields take the overnight trash shifts on the polo grounds, which the money chapter covers.462

In 2025, the TODEC Legal Center put up billboards of its own: ten of them, showing local farmworkers, around the valley during festival season, one of them featuring Pedro Zacarias of Thermal. TODEC counts more than 10,000 farmworkers in the valley. Its director, Luz Gallegos, said the festival pulls attention away from the east side, where the "real Coachella" is.490

Weather

The desert sets the calendar. The first Coachella, in October 1999, ran in temperatures over 100°F.3 Spring dates don't guarantee mild weather: in 2004, Saturday afternoon went past 100 degrees and was described as brutal.27 Wikipedia's summary of 2012 gives a range across the two weekends from 43°F on April 14 to 106°F on April 21.18 Every year, JFK Memorial Hospital in Indio, the closest hospital, adds staff for heat illness and drug and alcohol cases. Its medical director, Dr. Andrew Cassinove, said "many of these emergency visits are preventable."491

The bigger problem now is wind.

  • 2018. A high-wind warning, with gusts to 60 mph, held the weekend-two campground closed for about 18 hours. Thousands of campers waited in a Walmart lot in Indio and called it #Walmartchella.358 In La Quinta, sheriff's deputies moved campers out of another Walmart lot under a ban on sleeping in vehicles.359 The cities ended up publishing a list of 12 places to wait, including store lots, two casinos, a lake campground and La Quinta City Hall's south lot.360
  • 2019. On a windy April 21, a 120-pound light fixture fell from a tent ceiling and fractured a concertgoer's skull. She sued AEG and Goldenvoice that November.492
  • 2026, Coachella. On Friday, April 10, wind knocked a piece of equipment into the crowd during John Summit's set at the Do LaB, injuring a festivalgoer, and the stage closed for the night.241 Tents in the campgrounds were destroyed.483 Anyma's main-stage set was cancelled: "Due to strong wind conditions affecting Anyma's stage build, he is unable to perform tonight."241
  • 2026, Stagecoach. Two weeks later, the Stagecoach evacuation described above.90

What the season leaves behind

Three weekends of crowds leave a lot on the ground. A City of Indio environmental report found that Coachella, Stagecoach and Desert Trip attendees produced 107 tons of solid waste a day, with about 20% of it recycled.493 For 2022, the two spring festivals diverted 141 tonnes of mixed recycling and 86 tonnes of compost. Lund University's Kim Nicholas said the emissions from attendees and artists traveling in are "way bigger" than anything on site.494

The festival's own figures, which it reports itself, are larger. Its sustainability page claims 616,278 pounds composted or recycled across Coachella and Stagecoach in 2024, plus 79,120 pounds of materials donated to the Galilee Center and 56,051 pounds of food to the Coachella Valley Rescue Mission.140

The rest of the year

Since 2021, Goldenvoice has had the polo club year-round, not just for the festivals.455 And the land around it may not stay empty. In June 2025, the owner of Empire Polo Club proposed a mixed-use development on about 218 acres next to the festival grounds: homes, restaurants, shops and possibly a sports venue. County officials approved a key land-use change, but no designs or timeline had been released.495

The season, in other words, is getting longer. Desert X is moving into the fall. October has had rock and metal. August has pool parties. The festival is still the two weekends everyone plans around, but the valley has been filling in the calendar on either side.

Still open

  • Splash House's first year. 2013 or 2014? The McLeans would know.
  • Neon Carnival's first year. About 2007, by Bolthouse, or 2010, by BizBash's count.
  • Desert X in 2027-28. Whether a fall opening means billboard works will be up for both festival weekends of 2028.

Chapter 15

15The billboards on the I-10

The short versionEvery spring, artists and labels rent billboards on the interstate between Los Angeles and Indio, because there is one road in and the people driving it are exactly the people they want. The boards cost from about $8K to $40K for a few weeks, and one 2026 campaign reportedly ran to $1M. This is a sourced, year-by-year archive of them, from the first one on record in 2014 to the 20 that were booked for 2020 and never went up.

In April 2022, a few days before the festival that had dropped him as a headliner, Travis Scott's label put up a sequence of billboards along the I-10. The first said "Psst..." The next said "Looking for UTOPIA?" The third said "Wrong way!"496 Scott had been removed from the lineup after Astroworld. The boards were his way onto the road anyway.496

That is the whole idea in three signs. The festival has a lineup. The highway has a second one.

Why the desert

Coachella's own driving directions funnel almost everyone onto the I-10 into the valley.497 Gino Sesto, whose agency DASH TWO has sold Coachella boards since about 2015, put it simply to Billboard:

"It's kind of a perfect storm... you have one way in."498

Sam Keywanfar, whose company MilkMoney supplied billboard content for 26 advertisers that year, said the festivalgoer is "a very hard-to-reach demographic," and the drive is the chance to catch them "in the early part of the journey."498 Ian Dallimore of Lamar, one of the big billboard owners on the route, said artists get to advertise "on the road leading up to" the festival without advertising at it, and called a 14-by-48-foot roadside board "the biggest flex."498

The real target is the phone. Keywanfar again:

"When fans find a billboard they are drawn to and share it, that's the holy grail."498

The same Billboard piece describes artists stopping on the highway to take selfies with their own boards, and non-music brands, from Florence by Mills to the Elton John film Rocketman, following them onto the route the next year.498

Who owns them, who books them

Two owners come up again and again on this stretch. Outfront's own blog describes the I-10 run as "lined on either side with OUTFRONT billboards."499 Billboard calls Lamar "one of the top billboard operators on this stretch."498 Lamar also says it has had a Palm Springs-area operation since 2002; that is the company's own claim.500

The artists and labels mostly don't call them directly. They go through agencies and marketplaces: DASH TWO, a Los Angeles out-of-home agency; MilkMoney, which makes the content; and AdQuick, an online billboard marketplace, which says the inventory "is fragmented across multiple outdoor media owners" with no public map.498501

What it costs

The published prices, in order:

By the numbers · The going rate

  • 2019: $8,000-$20,000 for four weeks, sold out by January, with I-10 prices up 31% in late March and early April502
  • 2019: about $3M booked through AdQuick alone from February to April, across 378 boards it evaluated503
  • About 2022: $15,000-$40,000 per board, per MilkMoney's Keywanfar498
  • 2025-26: $10,000-$18,000 for four weeks on a standard board near Indio or La Quinta, $20,000 or more for digital boards or the Banning Pass and Cabazon, with a festival premium of 25-40%, per DASH TWO504
  • 2026: Addison Rae's campaign reportedly cost about $1M, per AdQuick501
  • 2026: about $900 to replace one vandalized vinyl panel505

Book early, the brokers say: six to nine months ahead, according to DASH TWO, or four to six, according to AdQuick.504501

Where they are

The boards cluster where the road narrows. Billboard describes about 50 miles of I-10 from Riverside to Indio, and Sesto estimated about 75 boards in the 23 miles from Banning to Palm Springs.498 OOH Today, using StreetMetrics data, counted more than 50 boards between Beaumont and Indio through the San Gorgonio Pass, and measured about 30% more impressions on them during each festival weekend of 2025.506

DASH TWO's own map of the route, which is a sales pitch and should be read as one, starts with the Banning Pass and Cabazon as the first boards drivers from LA and Orange County see, then the split where Highway 111 peels off toward Palm Springs, then the final mile near the polo club.504 The wind turbines are a landmark in their own right; in 2026 Slayyyter staged a bunny-suit stunt there.501

There is also a different kind of board off the interstate. The art biennial Desert X has put billboard works in the valley, starting in 2017 with a work by Jennifer Bolande on the road into Palm Springs.469 The season chapter covers those.

How it started

No one has documented a "first" Coachella billboard. The earliest found for this archive is in a DASH TWO blog post from January 2015, which shows the band Miami Horror "standing proudly in front of their billboard from last year," meaning 2014.507 The same post says only "a handful of billboards" were left for the 2015 festival.507 That is an agency talking about its own work, so it carries a low confidence label below.

Billboard's version is that the boards "first appeared about six years" before its piece, when labels started aiming at festivalgoers and at the performers driving the same road.498 The piece is undated, but it treats 2020 and 2021 as past and 2022 as current, which puts the start at about 2016. By 2019, the outdoor-ad trade body OAAA was writing up Billie Eilish's minimal text boards on the I-10.502

As for how many boards there are, AdQuick tracked 485 on the LA-to-festival route in 2018, 533 in 2019 and 548 in 2022.498 Those count every board on the route, not just festival campaigns.

2020: the boards that never went up

On March 10, 2020, Goldenvoice moved the April festival to October.49 In June, Riverside County cancelled it.50

DASH TWO had booked 20 Coachella billboards for 2020. Sesto told Billboard every one was cancelled.498 Which artists booked them, and whether any creative actually went up that spring, are both unknown. This project found no photo or report of a festival board standing on the I-10 with no festival behind it.

The industry around them fell hard. Out-of-home advertising revenue was down 45% year over year in the second quarter of 2020.508 Outfront's revenue for the year fell 30.6%.509 Lamar's fell 10.5%.510

2022: the comeback

When the festival came back, so did the boards, unevenly. DASH TWO booked 15, five fewer than its cancelled 2020 total. "There's a lot of skepticism still," Sesto said.498 Lamar sold 66 Coachella spots, against 64 in 2019, and Dallimore was upbeat.498

"We're seeing a lot of this business come back bigger, because people feel more comfortable."498

There were fewer faces to go around. Before the pandemic, local car dealers and casinos with year-round contracts sublet their boards to Coachella advertisers at a discount. After the 2020-21 cancellations, they stopped putting those boards in the deal.498

Scott's "Wrong way!" set the tone for what came next: boards built to be photographed, not just read. In 2025, a board reading "KIKI BOY 2025" went up with no explanation, and Rolling Stone asked whether Frank Ocean was teasing something.511 Not every board is welcome. In 2024, Goldenvoice demanded that the AIDS Healthcare Foundation take down its "Catch More Than Vibes?" board on the westbound I-10.512 And in 2026, enough boards were vandalized that officials planned stronger security for them.505

How this archive works

The list below is generated from the archive's data, one entry per board or campaign, grouped by year. The rules:

  • Every board has a source. Each entry says who reported it, what it showed, where it stood and what it promoted, as far as the sources say. Gaps are left as gaps.
  • Every board has a confidence label. High, medium or low, with a note on why. A board seen in several independent reports is high. An agency's own blog post, with no date or location, is low.
  • Photos are credited and linked. Official posts by artists, labels and billboard companies are embedded from where they were posted. Fan photos are used only with the photographer's written permission and credit. Nothing is downloaded and rehosted without permission. Where no image can be used, a text card describes the board and links to the source.
  • What gets hosted here. Only three kinds of image: photos taken from the road by Richard Lathrop (marked "From the road", with the date taken), Creative Commons photos with their author and license, and brand or label press-kit images. Everything else stays where it was posted, linked with credit. Boards known from the press or social posts with no usable photo yet are in the still-needed list at the bottom.
  • Street View is cited, not copied. Google's terms don't allow Street View screenshots for any purpose.513 So where an old Street View capture shows a board, the entry links to that view and gives Google's capture date, such as "Apr 2019," and the date it was checked. Street View is corroboration, not proof.
  • It is updated every spring. In 2026, The Desert Sun photographed the boards on March 31, ten days before the first weekend.514

Instagram roundups collect the boards every year too. They are a good place to look, but a roundup alone isn't enough for a high confidence label.

Send a sighting. Seen a board that isn't here, or have a better photo of one that is? Email hello@thirdpolararch.com with the photo, where you saw it (road, direction, nearest exit) and when. Say whether you'd like to be credited, and how. Either is fine.

Still open

  • Before 2014. A Hollywood Reporter headline, "The Road to Coachella Belongs to Republic Records," apparently from around 2013, suggests a label was on the road earlier. The page wouldn't load for this project. If you can read it, we'd like to know what it says.
  • The 20 boards of 2020. Who booked them, and whether any creative was ever printed.
  • April Street View captures. Which springs Google happened to drive the I-10 during festival season.

The archive

84 boards with at least one solid source, newest first. Every photo link goes to the original post or article and its credit. 19 of them are shown in photos taken from the road by Richard Lathrop, marked From the road. 16 more sightings rest on social posts alone - they're listed at the bottom until something firmer turns up.

'Catch More Than Vibes?' STD-testing billboard

"Catch More Than Vibes?" with FreeSTDCheck.org.

For: Free STD testing during the festival weekends

Tyre Nichols (photographs), Desert X 2023high

Originals

Six of Tyre Nichols' photographs (Memphis steel bridges and sunsets) on roadside billboards, with no added text reported. Desert X: 'the silent beauty of these levitated images stands in stark contrast with the terror.'

Where: North Gene Autry Trail between I-10 and Vista Chino, Palm Springs (Palm Springs Life). Getty/AFP caption says near Desert Hot Springs.

For: Tribute to Tyre Nichols, killed after a Memphis police traffic stop in January 2023. Supported by the City of Palm Springs Public Arts Commission.

After: On view March 4 - May 7, 2023, covering both Coachella weekends

2021

Xaviera Simmons (Desert X 2021)high

Because You Know Ultimately We Will Band A Militia

A series of billboards in standard ad typography with public-domain Met images and text statements. Quoted lines: 'You Are Entering the Reparations Framework'; 'California Once Tried to Ban Black People.'; 'You Keep Our Most Brilliant Minds in a Perpetual Loop of Articulating and Translating the Ramifications of Your Systemic Generational Plunder.'; 'We call for radical distribution of resources because economic precarity is another form of harm.' (beside a portrait of Marsha P. Johnson); 'Rupture your guilt amnesia.'

Where: Gene Autry Trail, Palm Springs, between Via Escuela and Interstate 10 (Time Out). PBS SoCal notes a Rocky's Pawn Shop billboard ('Make America Great') stands opposite.

For: Desert X 2021 (Mar 12 - May 16, 2021), held in the year Coachella was cancelled. Not a festival promo.

After: Temporary installation.

Sources: PBS SoCal Artbound - 'Palm Springs Billboards Prompt Heavy Road Trip Conversations' · Time Out Los Angeles - '6 installations to see at Desert X 2021' · LAist - Desert X returns with political statements

See it: photo (Lance Gerber (main + slideshows); Martin Mancha (Rocky's/installation gallery)) · photo (Lance Gerber, courtesy the artist and Desert X)

2019

Cara Romero (Desert X 2019)high

Jackrabbit, Cottontail & Spirits of the Desert

Enlarged photographs of four young Chemehuevi boys as 'time-traveling visitors' returning to ancestral lands; one shows a figure running in front of the Palm Springs wind turbines, another shows all four boys in headdresses beneath the text 'NO WALL'.

Where: Gene Autry Trail, Palm Springs - northbound between Via Escuela (also given as Vista Chino) and the 10 Freeway (Cactus Hugs). OAAA describes it as 'a billboard on the 10 Freeway'. Desert X lists coordinates 33.853965, -116.506052.

For: Desert X 2019 biennial (Feb 9 - Apr 21, 2019), overlapping Coachella 2019. Not a Coachella artist promo.

After: Temporary installation; ended with Desert X 2019.

Sources: The Spaces - 'Billboards bring Native American culture into the spotlight at Desert X' · Cactus Hugs - Desert X 2019 guide to the Jackrabbit billboard installation · OAAA blog · Desert X

See it: photo (James Mead for The Spaces) · photo (Desert X (no photographer named))

KYLE (SuperDuperKyle)medium

'Super Duper Kyle is going to the Moon' rocket-ship billboard

DASH TWO alt text: 'Retro rocket-ship billboard for KYLE, "Super Duper Kyle is going to the Moon," Friday 4:30 PM Main Stage'.

Where: Not pinned down. The post says DASH TWO's boards 'line the I-10 heading into Coachella' but does not say where this one stood.

For: KYLE's Friday Main Stage set at Coachella 2018

After: The vinyl from DASH TWO's 2018 Coachella boards went to Rareform to be cut into bags and accessories. A backpack printed with the KYLE rocket-ship art is pictured.

Sources: DASH TWO blog · Concert Archives

See it: photo (DASH TWO) · photo (DASH TWO / Rareform (backpack made from the board))

2017

Jennifer Bolande (Desert X art installation, not an artist promo)high

'Visible Distance / Second Sight' landscape billboards

No ad copy. Each billboard showed a photograph of the landscape it blocks, which lines up with the horizon as you approach.

Where: Gene Autry Trail in Palm Springs, at the Gene Autry Trail exit off the I-10 (LAist), on both northbound and southbound sides (Daily Bruin). Desert X gives Gene Autry Trail at Vista Chino (33.844917, -116.505839) and says to drive north from Vista Chino or south from the 10. Conflict: a Daily Bruin correction says the boards were not on Vista Chino; the Desert X archive uses Vista Chino as its reference point.

For: Desert X 2017 (February 25 - April 30, 2017), which overlapped Coachella 2017.

After: Removed after Desert X closed. Count conflict: the Daily Bruin first says six billboards, then says she planned six double-sided boards and ended up with three because of complications with Lamar Advertising.

Sources: LAist · Daily Bruin, 'UCLA professor's billboard art piece frames road to Coachella' · Desert X archive · Viewpoint Vancouver

See it: photo (Lance Gerber (per Desert X)) · photo (Lance Gerber (billboard photo); Kristie-Valerie Hoang/Daily Bruin (feature image)

'DAMN.' billboard on the road to Coachella

Not described. The post includes a time-lapse of the install.

Where: 'Roadside' on the way to Coachella. No road or town given.

Off-site festival parking (LNR Parking quoted; sponsor not named)medium

Coachella/Stagecoach off-site parking billboard on eastbound I-10

Advertises off-site parking for Coachella and Stagecoach at Avenue 48 and Van Buren in Coachella. It was changed to steer drivers to exit at Dillon Road instead of Monroe or Jefferson. Exact wording not reported.

Where: Facing eastbound I-10, Indio

For: Off-site festival parking lot (Avenue 48 & Van Buren, Coachella)

Sources: KESQ News Channel 3
